Chapter 3
Unitary Orbits and Functions

Unitary orbits of Hermitian operators with convex or concave functions

[
31
]

Abstract.

This short but self-contained survey presents a number of elegant matrix/operator inequalities for general convex or concave
functions, obtained with a unitary orbit technique. Jensen, sub or super-additivity type inequalities are considered. Some of them
are substitutes to classical inequalities (Choi, Davis, Hansen-Pedersen) for operator convex or concave functions. Various trace,
norm and determinantal inequalities are derived. Combined with an interesting decomposition for positive semi-definite matrices,
several results for partitioned matrices are also obtained.

Keywords: Operator inequalities, positive linear map, trace, unitary orbit, convex function,
symmetric norm, anti-norm.

AMS subjects classification 2010: Primary 15A60, 47A30, 47A60

3.1
Introduction

The functional analytic aspect of
Matrix Analysis is evident when matrices or operators are considered as non-commutative numbers, sequences or functions. In
particular, a significant part of this theory consists in establishing theorems for Hermitian matrices regarded
as generalized real numbers or functions. Two classical trace inequalities may illustrate quite well this assertion. Given two
Hermitian matrices
A
,
B
and a concave function
f(t)
defined on the real line,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)\geq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\frac{f(A)+f(B)}{2}

(3.1.1)

and, if further
f(0)\geq 0
and both
A
and
B
are positive semi-definite,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f(A+B)\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f(A)+{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f(B).

(3.1.2)

The first inequality goes back to von-Neumann in the 1920’s, the second is more subtle and has been proved only in 1969 by Rotfel’d

[
88
]
. These trace inequalities are matrix versions of obvious scalar inequalities.

The aim of this short survey is to present in a unified and self-contained way two recent significant improvement of the trace
inequalities (
3.1.1
)-(
3.1.2
) and some of their consequences. Our unitary orbit method is also used to prove some basic facts
such as the triangle inequality for Schatten
p
-norms or Minkowski’s determinantal inequality.

By operator, we mean a linear operator on a finite dimensional Hilbert space. We use interchangeably the terms operator and matrix.
Especially, a positive operator means a positive (semi-definite) matrix. Consistently
\mathbb{M}_{n}
denotes the set of operators on a space
of dimension
n
and
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
stands for the positive part. As many operator inequalities, our results lie in the scope of matrix
techniques. Of course, there are versions for operators acting on infinite dimensional, separable Hilbert spaces (and operator
algebras); we will indicate the slight modifications which might then be necessary.

The rest of this introduction explains why inequalities with unitary orbits are relevant for inequalities involving functional
calculus of operators such as the concavity-subadditivity statements (
3.1.1
) and (
3.1.2
).

That inequalities with unitary orbits naturally occur can be seen from the following two elementary facts. Firstly, If
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
are such that
A\geq B
(that is
A-B
is positive semi-definite) then, whenever
p>1
,
it does not follow in general that
A^{p}\geq B^{p}
. However, for any non-decreasing function
f(t)
, the eigenvalues (arranged in
decreasing order and counted with their multiplicities) of
f(A)
are greater or equal to the corresponding ones of
f(B)
. By the
min-max characterization of eigenvalues, this is equivalent to

f(A)\geq Uf(B)U^{*}

(3.1.3)

for some unitary
U\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Secondly, if
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
and
C\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
is a contraction, then we have
C^{*}AC\leq UAU^{*}
for some
unitary
U\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, i.e., the eigenvalues of
C^{*}AC
are smaller or equal to those of
A
. Note also that

C^{*}AC=VA^{1/2}CC^{*}A^{1/2}V^{*}\leq A
for some unitary
V
, since
TT^{*}
and
T^{*}T
are unitarily congruent for any operator
T
. The
reading of this paper does not require more knowledge about matrices, see
[
13
]
for a good background.

The most well-known matrix inequality involving unitary orbits is undoubtedly the triangle inequality due to Thompson
[
92
]
: If

X
and
Y
are two operators in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
, then

|X+Y|\leq U|X|U^{*}+V|Y|V^{*}

(3.1.4)

for some unitary
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Here
|X|:=(X^{*}X)^{1/2}
is the positive part of
X
occurring in the polar decomposition
X=V|X|

for some unitary
V
. By letting

X=\begin{pmatrix}A^{1/2}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad Y=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
B^{1/2}&0\end{pmatrix}

where
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, the triangle inequality (
3.1.4
) yields

\sqrt{A+B}\leq K\sqrt{A}K^{*}+L\sqrt{B}L^{*}

for some contractions
K,L\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Thus, for some unitaries

U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}

\sqrt{A+B}\leq U\sqrt{A}U^{*}+V\sqrt{B}V^{*}.

(3.1.5)

This inequality for the function
\sqrt{t}
is a special case of the main theorem of Section 3.

If
f(t)
is convex on
[0,\infty)
, then (
3.1.2
) is obviously reversed. In case of
f(t)=t^{p}
with exponents
p\in[1,2]
a much
stronger inequality holds,

\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)^{p}\leq\frac{A^{p}+B^{p}}{2},

(3.1.6)

this says that
t^{p}
is operator convex for
p\in[1,2]
, and this is no longer true if
p>2
. However by making use of (
3.1.3
)
and (
3.1.6
) we get, for any
p>1
,

\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)^{p}\leq U\frac{A^{p}+B^{p}}{2}U^{*},

(3.1.7)

for some unitary
U\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. In fact, if we assume that (
3.1.7
) holds for
p\in[2^{n},2^{n+1}]
,
n
a positive integer, then it
also holds for
2p\in[2^{n+1},2^{n+2}]
since

\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)^{2p}\leq U_{0}\left(\frac{A^{2}+B^{2}}{2}\right)^{p}U^{*}_{0}\leq U_{0}U_{1}\frac{A^{2p}+B^{2p}}{2}U_{1}^{*}U^{*}_{0}

for some unitary
U_{0},\,U_{1}
.
Inequality (
3.1.7
) may serve as a motivation for Section 2. It is worthwhile to notice that, in contrast with the theory of
operator convex functions, our methods are rather elementary.

3.2
A matrix Jensen type inequality

3.2.1
Jensen type inequalities via unitary orbits

In this section we present some extension of (
3.1.1
). The most general one involves a unital positive linear map. A linear
map
\Phi:\mathbb{M}_{n}\to\mathbb{M}_{d}
is unital if
\Phi(I)=I
where
I
stands for the identity of any order, and
\Phi
is positive if

\Phi(A)\in\mathbb{M}_{d}^{+}
for all
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
. The simplest case is given when
d=1
by the map

A\mapsto\langle h,Ah\rangle

(3.2.1)

for some unit vector
h
(our inner product is linear in the second variable). Restricting this map to the diagonal part (more
generally, to any commutative
*
-subalgebra) of
\mathbb{M}_{n}
, we have

A\mapsto\langle h,Ah\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\lambda_{i}(A)

(3.2.2)

where the
\lambda_{i}(A)
’s are the eigenvalues of the normal operator
A
and the
w_{i}
’s form a probability weight. For this reason,
unital positive linear maps are regarded as non-commutative versions of expectations. If
A
is Hermitian, and
f(t)
is a convex
function defined on the real line, the Jensen’s inequality may be written in term of the map
\Phi
in (
3.2.1
)-(
3.2.2
) as

f(\langle h,Ah\rangle)\leq\langle h,f(A)h\rangle.

(3.2.3)

The map (
3.2.1
) is a special case of a compression. Given an
n
-dimensional Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
and a
d
-dimensional
subspace
{\mathcal{S}}\subset\mathcal{H}
, we have a natural map from the algebra
{\mathrm{L}}(\mathcal{H})
of operators on

\mathcal{H}
onto the algebra
{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})
, the compression map onto
{\mathcal{S}}
,

A\mapsto A_{\mathcal{S}}:=EA_{|{\mathcal{S}}},\qquad A\in{\mathrm{L}}(\mathcal{H}),

where
E
denotes the ortho-projection onto
{\mathcal{S}}
. Identifying
{\mathrm{L}}(\mathcal{H})
with
\mathbb{M}_{n}
by picking an
orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathrm{L}}(\mathcal{S})
with
\mathbb{M}_{d}
via an orthonormal basis
{\mathcal{S}}
, we may
consider compressions as unital positive linear maps acting from
\mathbb{M}_{n}
onto
\mathbb{M}_{d}
, and they are then represented as

A\mapsto J^{*}AJ,\qquad A\in\mathbb{M}_{n},

where
J
is any
n
-by-
d
matrix such that
J^{*}J=I
, the identity of order
d
.

In view of (
3.2.3
) it is quite natural to compare for a convex function
f(A_{\mathcal{S}})
and
f(A)_{\mathcal{S}}
when
A

is a Hermitian on
\mathcal{H}
, i.e, a Hermitian in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. In this setting, the Jensen inequality (
3.2.3
) is adapted by
using unitary orbits on
\mathcal{S}
. This is actually true for any unital positive linear maps, as stated in Theorem 2.1 below.
This is the main result of this section.
The notation
\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
stands for the Hermitian part of
\mathbb{M}_{n}
with spectra in an interval
\Omega
of the real line.

Theorem 3.2.1
.

Let
\Phi:\mathbb{M}_{n}\to\mathbb{M}_{d}
be a unital positive linear map, let
f(t)
be a convex function on an
interval
\Omega
, and let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
. Then, for some unitary
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
,

f(\Phi(A))\leq\frac{U\Phi(f(A))U^{*}+V\Phi(f(A))V^{*}}{2}.

If furthermore
f(t)
is monotone, then we can take
U=V
. The inequality reverses for concave functions.

The next corollaries list some consequences of the theorem.
This statement for positive linear maps contains several Jensen type inequalities. The simplest one is obtained by taking
\Phi:\mathbb{M}_{2n}\to\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

\Phi\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
Y&B\end{bmatrix}\right):=\frac{A+B}{2}.

With
X=Y=0
, Theorem
3.2.1
then says:

Corollary 3.2.2
.

If
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
and
f(t)
is a convex function on an interval
\Omega
, then, for some
unitaries
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

f\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\{U\frac{f(A)+f(B)}{2}U^{*}+V\frac{f(A)+f(B)}{2}V^{*}\right\}.

If furthermore
f(t)
is monotone, then we can take
U=V
.

From this corollary we can get a generalization of the famous Minkowski inequality,

{\det}^{1/n}(A+B)\geq{\det}^{1/n}A+{\det}^{1/n}B,\qquad A,\,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}.

(3.2.4)

A proof is given after the proof of Corollary
3.2.10
below. Equivalently, (
3.2.4
) says that the Minkowski functional

X\mapsto\det^{1/n}X
is concave on the positive cone
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
.
Combined with the concave version of Corollary
3.2.2
, this concavity aspect of (
3.2.4
) is improved as:

Corollary 3.2.3
.

If
f(t)
is a non-negative concave function on an interval
\Omega
and if
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
, then,

{\det}^{1/n}f\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)\geq\frac{{\det}^{1/n}f(A)+{\det}^{1/n}f(B)}{2}.

Corollary
3.2.2
deals with the simplest convex combination, the arithmetic mean of two operators. Similar statements holds for
weighted means of several operators. In fact these means may even have operator weights (called
C^{*}
-convex combinations). An

m
-tuple
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
is an isometric column if
\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}^{*}Z_{i}=I
. We may then perform the
C^{*}
-convex
combination
\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}A_{i}Z_{i}
. If all the
A_{i}
’s
are Hermitian operators in
\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
for some interval
\Omega
, then so is
\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}A_{i}Z_{i}
. Hence, Corollary

3.2.2
is a very special case of the next one.

Corollary 3.2.4
.

Let
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be an isometric column in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
, let
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be in
\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
and
let
f(t)
be a convex function on
\Omega
. Then, for some unitary
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}A_{i}Z_{i}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\{U\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}f(A_{i})Z_{i}\right)U^{*}+V\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}f(A_{i})Z_{i}\right)V^{*}\right\}.

If furthermore
f(t)
is monotone, then we can take
U=V
. The inequality reverses for concave functions.

If all the
A_{i}
’s are zero except the first one, we obtain an inequality involving a congruence
Z_{1}^{*}A_{1}Z_{1}
with a contraction

Z_{1}
(that is
Z_{1}^{*}Z_{1}\leq I
). We state the concave version in the next corollary. It is a matrix version of the basic inequality

f(za)\geq zf(a)
for a concave function with
f(0)\geq 0
and real numbers
z,a
with
z\in[0,1]
.

Corollary 3.2.5
.

Let
f(t)
be a concave function on an interval
\Omega
with
0\in\Omega
and
f(0)\geq 0
, let
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
and let
Z
be a contraction in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Then, for some unitaries
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

f\left(Z^{*}AZ\right)\geq\frac{U\left(Z^{*}f(A)Z\right)U^{*}+V\left(Z^{*}f(A)Z\right)V^{*}}{2}.

If furthermore
f(t)
is monotone, then we can take
U=V
.

For a sub-unital positive linear map
\Phi
, i.e.,
\Phi(I)\leq I
, it is easy to see that Theorem
3.2.1
can be extended in the convex
case when
f(0)\leq 0
, and in the concave case, when
f(0)\geq 0
(this sub-unital version is proved in the proof of Corollary
3.2.7

below). This also contains Corollary
3.2.5
. The above results contains some inequalities for various norms and functionals, as
noted in some of the corollaries and remarks below. For instance we have the following Jensen trace inequalities.

Corollary 3.2.6
.

Let
f(t)
be a convex function defined on an interval
\Omega
, let
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be in

\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
, and let
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be an isometric column in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Then,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}A_{i}Z_{i}\right)\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}f(A)_{i}Z_{i}.

(3.2.5)

If further
0\in\Omega
and
f(0)\leq 0
, we also have

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f\left(Z^{*}_{1}A_{1}Z_{1}\right)\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}Z^{*}_{1}f(A_{1})Z_{1}.

(3.2.6)

A typical example of positive linear map on
\mathbb{M}_{n}
is the Schur multiplication map
A\mapsto Z\circ A
with an operator

Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
. Here
Z\circ A
is the entrywise product of
A
and
Z
. The fact that the Schur multiplication with
Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
is
a positive linear map can be easily checked by restricting the Schur product to positive rank ones operators.
Hence, Theorem
3.2.1
contains results for the Schur product. In particular, the sub-unital version yields:

Corollary 3.2.7
.

Let
f(t)
be a concave function on an interval
\Omega
with
0\in\Omega
and
f(0)\geq 0
, and let
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
. If
Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
has diagonal entries all less than or equal to 1, then, for some unitaries
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

f\left(Z\circ A\right)\geq\frac{U\left(Z\circ f(A)\right)U^{*}+V\left(Z\circ f(A)\right)V^{*}}{2}.

If furthermore
f(t)
is monotone, then we can take
U=V
.

Proof.

Let
\Psi:\mathbb{M}_{n}\to M_{d}
be a positive linear map and suppose that
\Psi
is sub-unital, i.e.,
\Psi(I)=C
for some
contraction
C\in\mathbb{M}_{d}^{+}
. Then the map
\Phi:\mathbb{M}_{n+1}\to M_{d}
,

\begin{bmatrix}A&\vdots\\
\ldots&b\end{bmatrix}\mapsto\Psi(A)+b(I-C)

is unital. Thus, by Theorem
3.2.1
, If
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{{\Omega}\}
where
\Omega
contains
0
and if
f(t)
is concave on
\Omega
,

f\left(\Phi(A\oplus 0)\right)\geq\frac{U\Phi(f(A\oplus 0))U^{*}+V\Phi(f(A\oplus 0))V^{*}}{2}

for some unitary
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
,
equivalently,

f(\Psi(A))\geq\frac{U\{\Psi(A)+f(0)(I-C)\}U^{*}+V\{\Psi(A)+f(0)(I-C)\}V^{*}}{2}

hence, if further
f(0)\geq 0
, the sub-unital form of Theorem
3.2.1
:

f(\Psi(A))\geq\frac{U\Psi(f(A))U^{*}+V\Psi(f(A))V^{*}}{2}.

Applying this to the sub-unital map
\Psi:A\mapsto Z\circ A
yields the corollary. ∎

Corollary
3.2.7
obviously contains a trace inequality companion to (
3.2.6
). By making use of (
3.2.4
) we
also have the next determinantal inequality.

Corollary 3.2.8
.

Let
f(t)
be a non-negative concave function on an interval
\Omega
,
0\in\Omega
, and let
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
. If
Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
has diagonal entries all less than or equal to 1, then,

\det f\left(Z\circ A\right)\geq\det Z\circ f(A).

(3.2.7)

Some other consequences of Theorem
3.2.1
are given below in Corollary
3.2.10
and in Subsection 2.2, as well as references and
related results.

We turn to the proof of Theorem
3.2.1
. Thanks to the next lemma, we will see that it is enough to prove Theorem
3.2.1
for compressions.
By an abelian
*
-subalgebra
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbb{M}_{m}
we mean a subalgebra containing the identity of
\mathbb{M}_{m}
and closed under the
involution
A\mapsto A^{*}
. Any abelian
*
-subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathbb{M}_{m}
is spanned by a total family of ortho-projections,
i.e., a family of mutually orthogonal projections adding up to the identity. A representation
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{M}_{n}
is a
unital linear map such
\pi(A^{*}B)=\pi^{*}(A)\pi(B)
.

Lemma 3.2.9
.

Let
\Phi
be a unital positive map from
an abelian
*
-subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathbb{M}_{n}

to the algebra
\mathbb{M}_{m}
identified as
{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})
. Then, there exists a space
{\mathcal{H}}\supset{\mathcal{S}}
,

\dim{\mathcal{H}}\leq nm
, and a
representation

\pi
from
{\mathcal{A}}
to
{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{H}})

such that

\Phi(X)=(\pi(X))_{\mathcal{S}}.

Proof.

{\mathcal{A}}
is generated by a total family of
k

projections
E_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,k
(say
E_{i}
are rank one, that is

k=n
). Let
A_{i}=\Phi(E_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,n
. Since
\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
is
the identity on
{\mathcal{S}}
, we can find operators
X_{i,j}
such
that

V=\begin{pmatrix}A_{1}^{1/2}&\dots&A_{n}^{1/2}\\
X_{1,1}&\dots&X_{n,1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
X_{1,n-1}&\dots&X_{n,n-1}\end{pmatrix}

is a unitary operator on
{\mathcal{F}}=\oplus^{n}{\mathcal{S}}
. Let
R_{i}
be the block matrix with the same
i
-th column than
V

and with all other entries
0
. Then, setting
P_{i}=R_{i}R_{i}^{*}
, we obtain a total family of projections on
{\mathcal{F}}
satifying

A_{i}=(P_{i})_{\mathcal{S}}
. We define
\pi
by
\pi(E_{i})=P_{i}
. ∎

In the following proof of Theorem
3.2.1
, and in the rest of the paper,
the eigenvalues of a Hermitian
X
on an
n
-dimensional space are denoted in non-increasing order as
\lambda_{1}(X)\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}(X)
.

Proof.

We consider the convex case. We first deal with a compression map. Hence
\mathbb{M}_{n}
is identified with
{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
and

\Phi(A)=A_{\mathcal{S}}

where
{\mathcal{S}}
is a subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}
.
We may find spectral subspaces
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime\prime}
for
A_{\mathcal{S}}
and a real
r
such that

(a)

{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime\prime}
,

(b)

the spectrum of
A_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}
lies on
(-\infty,r]
and the spectrum of
A_{\mathcal{S^{\prime\prime}}}
lies on
[r,\infty)
,

(c)

f
is monotone both on
(-\infty,r]\cap\Omega
and
[r,\infty)\cap\Omega
.

Let
k
be an integer,
1\leq k\leq\dim{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
. There exists a spectral subspace
{\mathcal{F}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
for

A_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}
(hence for
f(A_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}})
),
\dim{\mathcal{F}}=k
, such that

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}[f(A_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}})]

\displaystyle=\min_{h\in{\mathcal{F}};\ \|h\|=1}\langle h,f(A_{\mathcal{F}})h\rangle

\displaystyle=\min\{f(\lambda_{1}(A_{\mathcal{F}}))\,;\,f(\lambda_{k}(A_{\mathcal{F}}))\}

\displaystyle=\min_{h\in{\mathcal{F}};\ \|h\|=1}f(\langle h,A_{\mathcal{F}}h\rangle)

\displaystyle=\min_{h\in{\mathcal{F}};\ \|h\|=1}f(\langle h,Ah\rangle)

where at the second and third steps we use the monotony of
f
on
(-\infty,r]
and the fact that
A_{\mathcal{F}}
’s spectrum lies
on
(-\infty,r]
. The convexity of
f
implies

f(\langle h,Ah\rangle)\leq\langle h,f(A)h\rangle

for all normalized vectors
h
. Therefore, by the minmax principle,

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}[f(A_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}})]

\displaystyle\leq\min_{h\in{\mathcal{F}};\ \|h\|=1}\langle h,f(A)h\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{k}[f(A)_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}].

This statement is equivalent (by unitary congruence to diagonal matrices) to the existence of a unitary operator
U_{0}
on

{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
such that

f(A_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}})\leq U_{0}f(A)_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}U_{0}^{*}.

(Note that the monotone case is established.) Similarly we get a unitary
V_{0}
on
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime\prime}
such that

f(A_{\mathcal{S^{\prime\prime}}})\leq V_{0}f(A)_{\mathcal{S^{\prime\prime}}}V_{0}^{*}.

Thus we have

f(A_{\mathcal{S}})\leq\begin{pmatrix}U_{0}&0\\
0&V_{0}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}f(A)_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}&0\\
0&f(A)_{\mathcal{S^{\prime\prime}}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}U_{0}^{*}&0\\
0&V_{0}^{*}\end{pmatrix}.

Besides we note that, still in respect with the decomposition
{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime\prime}
,

\begin{pmatrix}f(A)_{\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}&0\\
0&f(A)_{\mathcal{S^{\prime\prime}}}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&I\end{pmatrix}f(A)_{\mathcal{S}}\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&I\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&-I\end{pmatrix}f(A)_{\mathcal{S}}\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&-I\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

So, letting

U=\begin{pmatrix}U_{0}&0\\
0&V_{0}\end{pmatrix}\quad{\rm and}\quad V=\begin{pmatrix}U_{0}&0\\
0&-V_{0}\end{pmatrix}

we get

f(A_{\mathcal{S}})\leq\frac{Uf(A)_{\mathcal{S}}U^{*}+Vf(A)_{\mathcal{S}}V^{*}}{2}

(3.2.8)

for some unitary
U,V\in{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})
, with
U=V
if
f(t)
is convex and monotone. This proves the case of
compression maps.

Next we turn to the case of a general unital linear map
\Phi:\mathbb{M}_{n}\to\mathbb{M}_{m}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be the abelian
*
-subalgebra of
\mathbb{M}_{n}
spanned by
A
. By restricting
\Phi
to
\mathcal{A}
and by identifying

\mathbb{M}_{m}
with
{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})
, Lemma
3.2.9
shows that
\Phi(X)=(\pi(X))_{\mathcal{S}}
for all
X\in\mathcal{A}
.
Since
f
and
\pi
commutes,
f(\pi(A))=\pi(f(A))
, we have from the compression case some unitary
U,V\in{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})=\mathbb{M}_{m}
such that,

\displaystyle f(\Phi(A))

\displaystyle=f((\pi(A))_{\mathcal{S}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{U(f(\pi(A))_{\mathcal{S}}U^{*}+V(f(\pi(A))_{\mathcal{S}}V^{*}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{U(\pi(f(A))_{\mathcal{S}}U^{*}+V(\pi(f(A))_{\mathcal{S}}V^{*}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{U\Phi(f(A))U^{*}+V\Phi(f(A))V^{*}}{2},

where we can take
U=V
if the function is convex and monotone.
∎

The following is an application of Theorem
3.2.1
to norm inequalities.
A norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{M}_{n}
is a symmetric norm if
\|A\|=\|UAV\|
for all
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and all unitary
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. These norms
are also called unitarily invariant norms. They contain the Schatten
p
-norms
\|\cdot\|_{p}
,
1\leq p<\infty
, defined as
\|A\|_{p}=\{\mathrm{Tr\,}|A|^{p}\}^{1/p}.

The polar decomposition shows that a symmetric norm
\|\cdot\|
is well defined by its value on the positive cone
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
. The map
on
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
,
A\mapsto\|A\|
is invariant under unitary congruence and is subadditive. There are also some interesting, related
superadditive functionals.
Fix
p<0
. The map
X\mapsto\|X^{p}\|^{1/p}
is continuous on the invertible part of
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
. If
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
is not invertible,
setting
\|X^{p}\|^{1/p}:=0
, we obtain a continuous map on
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
.

Corollary 3.2.10
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
and let
p<0
. Then, for all symmetric norms,

\|\,(A+B)^{p}\,\|^{1/p}\,\geq\,\|A^{p}\|^{1/p}+\|B^{p}\|^{1/p}.

(3.2.9)

Proof.

We will apply Theorem
3.2.1
to the monotone convex function on
(0,\infty)
,
t\mapsto t^{p}
.
First, assume that
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
are such that
\|A^{p}\|=\|B^{p}\|=1
and let
s\in[0,1]
. Then, thanks to Theorem
3.2.1
(or Corollary

3.2.4
),

\|(sA+(1-s)B)^{p}\|\leq\|sA^{p}+(1-s)B^{p}\|\leq s\|A^{p}\|+(1-s)\|B^{p}\|=1,

hence

\|(sA+(1-s)B)^{p}\|^{1/p}\geq 1.

(3.2.10)

Now, for general invertible
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, insert
A/\|A^{p}\|^{1/p}
and
B/\|B^{p}\|^{1/p}
in place of
A,B
in (
3.2.10
) and
take

s=\frac{\|A^{p}\|^{1/p}}{\|A^{p}\|^{1/p}+\|B^{p}\|^{1/p}}.

This yields (
3.2.9
).
∎

Corollary
3.2.10
implies Minkowski’s determinantal inequality (
3.2.4
). Indeed, in (
3.2.9
) take the norm on
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
defined by
\|A\|:=\frac{1}{n}{\mathrm{Tr\,}}A
,
and note that
\det^{1/n}A=\lim_{p\nearrow 0}\|A^{p}\|^{1/p}
.
Hence, the superadditivity of
A\mapsto\|A^{p}\|^{1/p}
for
p<0
entails the superadditivity of
A\mapsto\det^{1/n}A
.

If we apply Theorem
3.2.1
(or Corollary
3.2.2
) to the convex function on the real line
t\mapsto|t|
we obtain:
If
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}

are Hermitian, then

|A+B|\leq\frac{U(|A|+|B|)U^{*}+V(|A|+|B|)V^{*}}{2}

for some unitaries
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
.
In fact, we can take
U=I
and this remains true for normal operators
A,B
. This is shown in the
proof of the following proposition.

Proposition 3.2.11
.

If
f(t)
is a nondecreasing convex function on
[0,\infty)
and if
Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
has a Cartesian decomposition
Z=A+iB
,
then, for some unitaries
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

f(|Z|)\leq\frac{Uf(|A|+|B|)U^{*}+Vf(|A|+|B|)V^{*}}{2}.

Proof.

let
X
,
Y
be two normal operators in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Then, the following operators in
\mathbb{M}_{2n}
are positive
semi-definite,

\begin{pmatrix}|X|&X^{*}\\
X&|X|\end{pmatrix}\geq 0,\qquad\begin{pmatrix}|Y|&Y^{*}\\
Y&|Y|\end{pmatrix}\geq 0,

and consequently

\begin{pmatrix}|X|+|Y|&X^{*}+Y^{*}\\
X+Y&|X|+|Y|\end{pmatrix}\geq 0.

Next, let
W
be the unitary part in the polar decomposition
X+Y=W|X+Y|
. Then

\begin{pmatrix}I&-W^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}|X|+|Y|&X^{*}+Y^{*}\\
X+Y&|X|+|Y|\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I\\
-W\end{pmatrix}\geq 0,

that is

|X|+|Y|+W^{*}(|X|+|Y|)W-2|X+Y|\geq 0.

Equivalently,

|X+Y|\leq\frac{|X|+|Y|+W^{*}(|X|+|Y|)W}{2}.

(3.2.11)

Letting
X=A
and
Y=iB
, and applying
f(t)
to both sides of (
3.2.11
),
Corollary 2.2 completes the proof since
f(t)
is nondecreasing and convex.
∎

Proposition 3.2.12
.

If
f(t)
is a nondecreasing convex function on
[0,\infty)
and if
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
are Hermitian, then, for some
unitaries
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

f((A+B)_{+})\leq\frac{Uf(A_{+}+B_{+})U^{*}+Vf(A_{+}+B_{+})V^{*}}{2}.

Proof.

Here
A_{+}:=(A+|A|)/2
.
Note that
A+B\leq A_{+}+B_{+}
. Let
E
be the projection onto
{\mathrm{ran\,}}(A+B)_{+}
and let
F
be the projection onto

\ker(A+B)_{+}

Since

(A+B)_{+}=E(A+B)E
, we have

(A+B)_{+}\leq E(A_{+}+B_{+})E+F(A_{+}+B_{+})F,

equivalently

(A+B)_{+}\leq\frac{(A_{+}+B_{+})+W(A_{+}+B_{+})W^{*}}{2}

(3.2.12)

where
W=E-F
is a unitary. Applying Corollary 2.2 completes the proof.
∎

3.2.2
Comments and references

In this second part of Section 2, we collect few remarks which complete Theorem
3.2.1
and the above corollaries. Good references for
positive maps and operator convex functions are the nice survey and book
[
64
]
and
[
13
]
.

Remark 3.2.13
.

Theorem
3.2.1
appears in
[
23
]
. It is stated therein for compressions maps and for the case of or
*
-convex
combinations given in Corollaries
3.2.4
and
3.2.5
(the monotone case was earlier obtained in
[
22
]
). That the compression case
immediately entails the general case of an arbitrary unital positive map is mentioned in some subsequent papers, for instance in

[
12
]
where some inequalities for Schur products are pointed out. From the Choi-Kraus representation of completely positive
linear maps, readers with a background on positive maps may also notice that Corollary
3.2.5
and Theorem
3.2.1
are equivalent.
For scalar convex combinations and with the assumption that
f(t)
is non-decreasing, Theorem
3.2.1
is first noted in Brown-Kosaki’s
paper
[
50
]
; with these assumptions, it is also obtained in Aujla-Silva’s paper
[
11
]
.

Remark 3.2.14
.

Let
g(t)
denote either the convex function
t\mapsto|t|
or
t\mapsto t_{+}
. Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
be Hermitian. Then
(2.11) and (2.12) show that

g\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)-\frac{g(A)+g(B)}{4}\leq V\frac{g(A)+g(B)}{4}V^{*}

for some unitary
V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. It would be interesting to characterize convex functions for which such a relation holds.

Remark 3.2.15
.

Theorem
3.2.1
holds for operators acting on infinite dimensional spaces, with an additional
rI
term. We state here the
monotone version.
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
are two separable Hilbert spaces and
r>0
is fixed.
Let
\Phi:{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
be a unital positive linear map, let
f(t)
be a monotone
convex function on
(-\infty,\infty)
and let

A,B\in{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
be Hermitian. Then, for some unitary
U\in{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})
,

f(\Phi(A))\leq U\Phi(f(A))U^{*}+rI.

(3.2.13)

The proof is given in the first author’s thesis when
\Phi
is a compression map, this entail the general case. For convenience, the
proof is given at the end of this section.

Remark 3.2.16
.

The trace inequality (
3.2.5
) is due to Hansen-Pedersen
[
63
]
, and the special case (
3.2.6
) is due to Brown-Kosaki.
Corollary
3.2.4
considerably improves (
3.2.5
): In case of a monotony assumption on the convex function
f(t)
, we have eigenvalue
inequalities; and, in the general case we may still infer the majorization relation

\sigma_{k}\left[f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}A_{i}Z_{i}\right)\right]\leq\sigma_{k}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}f(A)_{i}Z_{i}\right],\qquad k=1,\ldots,n,

(3.2.14)

where
\sigma_{k}[X]:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}[X]
is the sum of the
k
largest eigenvalues of a Hermitian
X
. In fact, the basic
relation
\sigma_{k}[X]=\max{\mathrm{Tr\,}}XE
, where the maximum runs over all rank
k
projections
E
, shows that

\sigma_{k}[\cdot]
is convex, increasing on the Hermitian part of
\mathbb{M}_{n}
so that (
3.2.14
) is an immediate consequence of Theorem

3.2.1
. The theorem also entails (see
[
23
]
for details) a rather unexpected eigenvalue inequality:

\lambda_{2k-1}\left[f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}A_{i}Z_{i}\right)\right]\leq\lambda_{k}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}f(A)_{i}Z_{i}\right],\qquad 1\leq k\leq(n+1)/2.

Remark 3.2.17
.

Choi’s inequality
[
52
]
claims:
for an operator convex function
f(t)
on
\Omega
,

f(\Phi(A))\leq\Phi(f(A))

(3.2.15)

for all
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
and all unital positive linear map.
Thus
Theorem
3.2.1
is a substitute of Choi’s inequality for a general convex function. In the special case of a compression map, then
(
3.2.15
) is Davis’ inequality
[
56
]
, a famous characterization of operator convexity. The most well-known case of Davis’
inequality is for the inverse map on positive definite matrices, it is then an old classical fact of Linear Algebra. Exactly as
Theorem
3.2.1
entails Corollary
3.2.4
, Choi’s inequality contains
Hansen-Pedersen’s inequality
[
62
]
,
[
63
]
:
If
f(t)
is operator convex on
\Omega
, then

f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}A_{i}Z_{i}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z^{*}_{i}f(A)_{i}Z_{i}

for all unitary columns
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
and
A_{i}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
.

For operator concave functions, the inequality reverses. A special case is Hansen’s inequality
[
61
]
:
if
f(t)
is
operator concave on
\Omega
,
0\in\Omega
and
f(0)\geq 0
, then

f(Z^{*}AZ)\geq Z^{*}f(A)Z

(3.2.16)

for all
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}\{\Omega\}
and all contractions
Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
.

Remark 3.2.18
.

Hansen’s inequality (
3.2.16
) may be formulated with an expansive operator
Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, i.e.,
Z^{*}Z\geq I
; then
(
3.2.16
) obviously reverses. We might expect that in a similar way, Corollary
3.2.5
or the Brown-Kosaki trace inequality
reverses. But this does not hold. Corollary
3.2.5
can not reverse when
Z
is expansive, even under the monotony assumption on
f(t)
.
An unexpected positivity assumption is necessary, and we must confine to weaker inequalities, such as trace inequalities:
if

f(t)
is a concave function on the positive half-line with
f(0)\geq 0
, then,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f\left(Z^{*}AZ\right)\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}Z^{*}f(A)Z

for all
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
and all expansive
Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
.
For a proof, see
[
22
]
and also
[
24
]
,
[
30
]
where remarkable
extensions to norm inequalities are given.

Remark 3.2.19
.

Lemma
3.2.9
is a part of Stinespring’ s theory of positive and completely positive linear maps in the influential 1955
paper
[
90
]
. The proof given here is somewhat original
and is taken from
[
12
]
. Note that in the course of the proof, we prove Naimark’s dilation theorem:
If
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are
positive operators on a space
{\mathcal{S}}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\leq I
, then there exist some mutually orthogonal
projections
\{P_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
on a larger space
{\mathcal{H}}\supset{\mathcal{S}}
such that
(P_{i})_{\mathcal{S}}=A_{i}
, (
1\leq i\leq n
).

Remark 3.2.20
.

Given a symmetric norm
\|\cdot\|
and
p<0
, the functionals defined on
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
,

A\mapsto\|A^{p}\|^{1/p}
, are introduced in
[
29
]
and called
derived anti-norms
. Corollary
3.2.10
is given therein,

[
29
, Proposition 4.6]
. The above proof is much simpler than the original one. For more details and many results on
anti-norms
and
derived anti-norms
, often in connection with Theorem
3.2.1
, see
[
28
]
and
[
29
]
. Several results in
these papers are generalizations of Corollary
3.2.4
. By using (
3.1.7
) and arguing as in the proof of Corollary
3.2.10
, we may derive the
triangle inequality for the Schatten
p
-norms on
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, i.e.,

\{{\mathrm{Tr}\,}(A+B)^{p}\}^{1/p}\leq\{{\mathrm{Tr\,}}A^{p}\}^{1/p}+\{{\mathrm{Tr\,}}B^{p}\}^{1/p},\qquad A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+},\ p>1.

(3.2.17)

Remark 3.2.21
.

The inequality (
3.2.11
) for normal operators can be extended to general
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, with a similar proof, as

|A+B|\leq\frac{|A|+|B|+V(|A^{*}|+|B^{*}|)V^{*}}{2}

(3.2.18)

for some unitary
V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. This is pointed out in
[
48
]
. This is still true for operators
A,B
in a von Neumann algebra

\mathcal{M}
with
V
a partial isometry in
\mathcal{M}
. If
\mathcal{M}
is endowed with a regular trace, this gives a short,
simple proof of the triangle inequality for the trace norm on
\mathcal{M}
.
Inequality (
3.2.11
) raises the question of comparison
|A+B|
and
|A|+|B|
. The following result is given in
[
76
]
.

Let
A_{1},\cdots,A_{m}
be invertible operators with
condition numbers dominated by
\omega>0
. Then

|A_{1}+\cdots+A_{m}|\leq\frac{\omega+1}{2\sqrt{\omega}}(|A_{1}|+\cdots+|A_{m}|).

Here the condition number of an invertible operator
A
on a Hilbert space is
\|A\|\|A^{-1}\|^{-1}
. Note that the bound is
independent of the number of operators. Though it is a rather low bound, it is not known whether it is sharp. Combining (
3.2.18
)
and (
3.2.17
) we get the triangle inequality for the Schatten
p
-norms on the whole space
\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

\{{\mathrm{Tr}\,}|A+B|^{p}\}^{1/p}\leq\{{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|A|^{p}\}^{1/p}+\{{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|B|^{p}\}^{1/p},\qquad A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n},\ p>1.

It remains to give a proof of the infinite dimensional version (
3.2.13
) of the monotone case of Theorem 2.1, the non-monotone
case following in a similar way to the finite dimensional version. As for finite dimensional spaces, we may assume that
\Phi
is a
compression map, thus we consider a subspace
\mathcal{S}\subset{\mathcal{H}}
and the map
A\mapsto A_{\mathcal{S}}
. By replacing

f(t)
by
f(-t)
and
A
by
-A
, we may also assume that
f(t)
is nondecreasing.

If
X
is a Hermitian on
\mathcal{H}
, we define a sequence of numbers
\{\lambda_{k}(X)\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
,

\lambda_{k}(X)=\sup_{\{{\cal F}\,:\,\dim{\cal F}=k\}}\,\inf_{\{h\in{\cal F}\,:\,\|h\|=1\}}\langle h,Xh\rangle

where the supremum runs over
k
-dimensional subspaces. Note that
\{\lambda_{k}(X)\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
is a non-increasing sequence
whose limit is the upper bound of the essential spectrum of
X
. We also define
\{\lambda_{-k}(X)\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
,

\lambda_{-k}(X)=\sup_{\{{\cal F}\,:\,{\rm codim\,}{\cal F}=k-1\}}\,\inf_{\{h\in{\cal F}\,:\,\|h\|=1\}}\langle h,Xh\rangle.

Then,
\{\lambda_{-k}(X)\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
is a nondecreasing sequence whose limit is the lower bound of the essential spectrum of

X
. The following fact (a) is obvious and fact (b) is easily checked.

(a)

If
X\leq Y
, then
\lambda_{k}(X)\leq\lambda_{k}(Y)
and
\lambda_{-k}(X)\leq\lambda_{-k}(Y)
for all
k=1,\cdots
.

(b)

if
r>0
and
X,\,Y
are Hermitian,
\lambda_{k}(X)\leq\lambda_{k}(Y)
and
\lambda_{-k}(X)\leq\lambda_{-k}(Y)
,for all

k=1,\dots
, then
X\leq UYU^{*}+rI
for some unitary
U
.

These facts show that, given
r>0
, two Hermitians
X
,
Y
with
X\leq Y
, and a continuous nondecreasing function
\phi
, there
exists a unitary
U
such that
\phi(X)\leq U\phi(Y)U^{*}+rI
.

By fact (b) it suffices to show that

\lambda_{k}(f(A_{\cal S}))\leq\lambda_{k}(f(A)_{\cal S})

(3.2.19)

and

\lambda_{-k}(f(A_{\cal S}))\leq\lambda_{-k}(f(A)_{\cal S})

(3.2.20)

for all
k=1,\cdots
. Now, we prove (
3.2.20
) and distinguish two cases:

1.
\lambda_{-k}(A_{\cal S})
is an eigenvalue of
A_{\cal S}
. Then, for
1\leq j\leq k
,
\lambda_{-j}(f(A_{\cal S}))
are
eigenvalues for
f(A_{\cal S})
. Consequently, there exists a subspace

{\cal F}\subset{\cal S}
,
{\rm codim}_{\cal S}\,{\cal F}=k-1
, such that

\displaystyle\lambda_{-k}(f(A_{\cal S}))=

\displaystyle\min_{\{h\in{\cal F}\,:\,\|h\|=1\}}\langle h,f(A_{\cal S})h\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\{h\in{\cal F}\,:\,\|h\|=1\}}f(\langle h,A_{\cal S}h\rangle)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\inf_{\{h\in{\cal F}\,:\,\|h\|=1\}}\langle h,f(A)h\rangle\leq\lambda_{-k}(f(A)_{\cal S})

where we have used that
f
is non-decreasing and convex.

2.
\lambda_{-k}(A_{\cal S})
is not an eigenvalue of
A_{\cal S}
(so,
\lambda_{-k}(A_{\cal S})
is the lower bound of the
essential spectrum of
A_{\cal S}
).
Fix
\varepsilon>0
and choose
\delta>0

such that
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq\varepsilon
for all
x
,
y
are in the convex hull of the spectrum of
A
with
|x-y|\leq\delta
. There
exists a subspace
{\cal F}\subset{\cal S}
,
{\rm codim}_{\cal S}\,{\cal F}=k-1
, such that

\lambda_{-k}(A_{\cal S})\leq\inf_{\{h\in{\cal F}\,:\,\|h\|=1\}}\langle h,A_{\cal S}h\rangle+\delta.

Since
f
is continuous nondecreasing we have
f(\lambda_{-k}(A_{\cal S}))=\lambda_{-k}(f(A_{\cal S}))
so that, as
f
is
nondecreasing,

\lambda_{-k}(f(A_{\cal S}))\leq f\left(\inf_{\{h\in{\cal F}\,:\,\|h\|=1\}}\langle h,A_{\cal S}h\rangle+\delta\right).

Consequently,

\lambda_{-k}(f(A_{\cal S}))\leq\inf_{\{h\in{\cal F}\,:\,\|h\|=1\}}f(\langle h,A_{\cal S}h\rangle)+\varepsilon,

so, using the convexity of
f
and the definition of
\lambda_{-k}(\cdot)
, we get

\lambda_{-k}(f(A_{\cal S}))\leq\lambda_{-k}(f(A)_{\cal S})+\varepsilon.

By letting
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
, the proof of (
3.2.20
) is complete. The proof of (
3.2.19
) is similar.
Thus (
3.2.13
) is established.

3.3
A matrix subadditivity inequality

3.3.1
Sub/super-additivity inequalities via unitary orbits

This section deals with some recent subadditive properties for concave functions, and similarly superadditive properties of convex
functions. The main result is:

Theorem 3.3.1
.

Let
f(t)
be a monotone concave function on
[0,\infty)
with
f(0)\geq 0
and let

A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
. Then, for some unitaries
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

f(A+B)\leq Uf(A)U^{*}+Vf(B)V^{*}.

Thus, the obvious scalar inequality
f(a+b)\leq f(a)+f(b)
can be extended to positive matrices
A
and
B
by considering element in
the unitary orbits of
f(A)
and
f(B)
. This inequality via unitary orbits considerably improves the famous Rotfel’d trace
inequality (
3.1.2
) for a non-negative concave function on the positive half-line,
and its symmetric norm version

\|f(A+B)\|\leq\|f(A)\|+\|f(B)\|

(3.3.1)

for all
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
and all symmetric norms
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{M}_{n}
.

Of course Theorem
3.3.1
is equivalent to the next statement for convex functions:

Corollary 3.3.2
.

Let
g(t)
be a monotone convex function on
[0,\infty)
with
g(0)\leq 0
and let

A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
. Then, for some unitaries
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

g(A+B)\geq Ug(A)U^{*}+Vg(B)V^{*}.

(3.3.2)

Proof.

It suffices to prove the convex version, Corollary
3.3.2
. We may confine the proof to the case
g(0)=0
as if
(
3.3.2
) holds for a function
g(t)
then it also holds for
g(t)-\alpha
for any
\alpha>0
. This assumption combined with the
monotony of
g(t)
entails that
g(t)
has a constant sign
\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}
, hence
g(t)=\varepsilon|g|(t)
.

We may also assume that
A+B
is invertible. Then

A=X(A+B)X^{*}\quad{\rm and}\quad B=Y(A+B)Y^{*}

where

X=A^{1/2}(A+B)^{-1/2}
and
Y=B^{1/2}(A+B)^{-1/2}
are
contractions. For any
T\in\mathbb{M}_{n},

T^{*}T
and
TT^{*}
are unitarily
congruent. Hence, using Corollary
3.2.5
we have two unitary operators
U_{0}
and
U
such that

\displaystyle g(A)

\displaystyle=g(X(A+B)X^{*})

\displaystyle\leq U_{0}Xg(A+B)X^{*}U_{0}^{*}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon U^{*}(|g|(A+B))^{1/2}X^{*}X(|g|(A+B))^{1/2}U,

so,

Ug(A)U^{*}\leq\varepsilon(|g|(A+B))^{1/2}X^{*}X(|g|(A+B))^{1/2}.

(3.3.3)

Similarly there exists a unitary operator
V
such that

Vg(B)V^{*}\leq\varepsilon(|g|(A+B))^{1/2}Y^{*}Y(|g|(A+B))^{1/2}.

(3.3.4)

Adding (3.3) and (3.4) we get

Ug(A)U^{*}+Vg(B)V^{*}\leq g(A+B)

since
X^{*}X+Y^{*}Y=I.
∎

The following corollary is matrix version of another obvious scalar inequality.

Corollary 3.3.3
.

Let
f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be concave and let

A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
be Hermitian.
Then, for some unitaries
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

Uf(A)U^{*}-Vf(B)V^{*}\leq f(|A-B|).

Proof.

Note that

A\leq|A-B|+B.

Since
f(t)
is non-decreasing and concave there exists unitaries
W,\,S,\,T
such that

Wf(A)W^{*}\leq f(|A-B|+B)\leq Sf(|A-B|)S^{*}+Tf(B)T^{*}.

Hence, we have

Uf(A)U^{*}-Vf(B)V^{*}\leq f(|A-B|)

for some unitaries
U,\,V.
∎

We can employ Theorem
3.3.1
to get an elegant inequality for positive block-matrices,

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{M}_{n+m}^{+},\qquad A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+},\,B\in\mathbb{M}_{m}^{+},

which nicely extend (
3.3.1
). To this end we need an interesting
decomposition lemma for elements in
\mathbb{M}_{n+m}^{+}
.

Lemma 3.3.4
.

For every matrix in
\mathbb{M}_{n+m}^{+}
written in blocks, we have a decomposition

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}=U\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}U^{*}+V\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}V^{*}

(3.3.5)

for some unitaries
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n+m}
.

Proof.

To obtain this decomposition of the positive semi-definite block matrix, factorize it as a square of positive matrices,

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}C&Y\\
Y^{*}&D\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}C&Y\\
Y^{*}&D\end{bmatrix}

and observe that it can be written as

\begin{bmatrix}C&0\\
Y^{*}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}C&Y\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0&Y\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
Y^{*}&D\end{bmatrix}=T^{*}T+S^{*}S.

Then, use the fact that
T^{*}T
and
S^{*}S
are unitarily congruent to

TT^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad SS^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix},

completing the proof of the decomposition.
∎

Combined with Theorem
3.3.1
, the lemma yields a norm inequality for block-matrices. A symmetric norm on
\mathbb{M}_{n+m}
induces a
symmetric norm on
\mathbb{M}_{n}
, via
\|A\|=\|A\oplus 0\|
.

Corollary 3.3.5
.

Let
f(t)
be a non-negative concave
function on
[0,\infty)
. Then, given an arbitrary partitioned
positive semi-definite matrix,

\left\|\,f\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)\right\|\leq\left\|f(A)\right\|+\left\|f(B)\right\|

for all symmetric norms.

Proof.

From (
3.3.5
) and Theorem
3.3.1
, we have

f\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)=U\begin{bmatrix}f(A)&0\\
0&f(0)I\end{bmatrix}U^{*}+V\begin{bmatrix}f(0)I&0\\
0&f(B)\end{bmatrix}V^{*}

for some unitaries
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n+m}
. The result then follows from the simple fact
that symmetric norms are nondecreasing functions of the singular values.
∎

Applied to
X=A^{1/2}B^{1/2}
, this result yields the Rotfel’d type inequalities (
3.1.2
)-(
3.3.1
), indeed,

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}A^{1/2}&0\\
B^{1/2}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A^{1/2}&B^{1/2}\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}

is then unitarily equivalent to
(A+B)\oplus 0
.
In case of the trace norm, the above result may be restated as a trace inequality without any non-negative assumption:
For all
concave functions
f(t)
on the positive half-line and all positive block-matrices,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f(A)+{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f(B).

The case of
f(t)=\log t
then gives Fisher’s inequality,

\det\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\leq\det A\det B.

Theorem
3.3.1
may be used to extend another classical (superadditive and concavity) property of the determinant, the Minkowski
inequality (
3.2.4
). We have the following extension:

Corollary 3.3.6
.

If
g:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a convex function,
g(0)=0
,
and
A,\,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, then,

{\det}^{1/n}g(A+B)\geq{\det}^{1/n}g(A)+{\det}^{1/n}g(B).

As another example of combination of Theorem
3.3.1
and (
3.3.5
), we have:

Corollary 3.3.7
.

Let
f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be concave and let
A=(a_{i,j})

be a positive semi-definite matrix in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Then, for some
rank one ortho-projections
\{E_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

f(A)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(a_{i,i})E_{i}.

Proof.

By a limit argument, we may assume that
A
is invertible, and hence we may also assume that

f(0)=0
,
indeed if the spectrum of
A
lies in an interval
[r,s]
,
r>0
, we may replace
f(t)

by any concave function on

[0,\infty)
such that
\tilde{f}(0)=0
and
\tilde{f}(t)=f(t)
for

t\in[r,s]
. By a repetition of (
3.3.5
) we have

A=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i,i}F_{i}

for some
rank one ortho-projections
\{F_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. An application of Theorem
3.3.1
yields

f(A)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}f(a_{i,i}F_{i})U_{i}^{*}

for some unitary operators
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Since
f(0)=0
, for each
i
,
U_{i}f(a_{i,i}F_{i})U_{i}^{*}=f(a_{i,i})E_{i}
for some rank one
projection
E_{i}
.
∎

Corollary
3.3.7
refines the standard majorization inequality relating a positive semi-definite
n
-by-
n
matrix and its diagonal part,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f(A)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}f(a_{i,i}).

3.3.2
Comments and references

Remark 3.3.8
.

Theorem
3.3.1
, Corollaries
3.3.2
and
3.3.2
are from
[
12
]
. In case of positive operators acting on an infinite
dimensional, separable Hilbert space, we have a version of Theorem
3.3.1
with an additional
r
I term in the RHS, as in (2.13).

Remark 3.3.9
.

The decomposition of a positive block-matrix in Lemma
3.3.4
is due to the authors. It is used in
[
75
]
to obtain
the norm inequality stated in Corollary
3.3.5
. The next two Corollaries
3.3.6
and
3.3.7
are new, though already announced in
[
28
]
.

Remark 3.3.10
.

The concavity requirement on
f(t)
in Rotfel’d inequality (
3.1.2
) and hence in Theorem
3.3.1
cannot be relaxed to a mere
superadditivity assumption; indeed take for
s,t>0
,

A=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}s&\sqrt{st}\\
\sqrt{st}&t\end{bmatrix},\qquad B=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}s&-\sqrt{st}\\
-\sqrt{st}&t\end{bmatrix},

and observe that the trace inequality
\mathrm{Tr\,}f(A+B)\leq\mathrm{Tr\,}f(A)+f(B)

combined with
f(0)=0
means that
f(t)
is concave.

Remark 3.3.11
.

There exists a norm version of Rotfel’d inequality which considerably improves (
3.3.1
).
If

f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is concave and
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, then

\|f(A+B)\|\leq\|f(A)+f(B)\|

for all symmetric norms.
The case of operator concave functions is given in
[
6
]
and the general case is established in

[
49
]
, see also
[
30
]
for further results. Concerning differences, the following inequality holds

\|f(A)-f(B)\|\leq\|f(|A-B|)\|

for all symmetric norms,
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, and operator monotone functions
f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
. This is a famous result of
Ando
[
4
]
. Here the operator monotonicity assumption is essential, see
[
10
]
for some counterexamples. A very interesting
paper by Mathias
[
79
]
gives a direct proof, without using the integral representation of operator monotone functions.

Remark 3.3.12
.

There exists also some subaditivity results involving convex functions
[
28
]
. For instance:

Let
g(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}a_{k}t^{k}
be a polynomial of degree
m
with all non-negative
coefficients. Then, for all positive operators
A,\,B
and all symmetric norms,

\|g(A+B)\|^{1/m}\leq\|g(A)\|^{1/m}+\|g(B)\|^{1/m}.

Remark 3.3.13
.

It is not known wether the monotonicity assumption in Theorem 3.1 can be deleted, i.e., wether the theorem holds for
all concave functions
f(t)
on
[0,\infty)
with
f(0)\geq 0
.

3.4
Around this article

We will see several applications of this article in the next chapters. Here we recall some results from
[
40
]
improving the Clarkson-McCarthy inequalities
for
p\geq 2
. These inequalities show that the unit ball for the Schatten
p
-norm is uniformly convex,

\left\|\frac{A+B}{2}\right\|_{p}^{p}+\left\|\frac{A-B}{2}\right\|_{p}^{p}\leq\frac{\|A\|_{p}^{p}+\|B\|_{p}^{p}}{2},\quad p\geq 2,

i.e.,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\left|\frac{A+B}{2}\right|^{p}+{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\left|\frac{A-B}{2}\right|^{p}\leq\frac{{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|A|^{p}+{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|B|^{p}}{2},\quad p\geq 2.

(3.4.1)

Thus, if
\|A\|_{p}=\|B\|_{p}=1
and
\|A-B\|_{p}=\varepsilon
,

\left\|\frac{A+B}{2}\right\|_{p}\leq\left(1-(\varepsilon/2)^{p}\right)^{1/p},\quad p\geq 2,

which estimates the uniform convexity modulus of the Schatten
p
-classes for
p\geq 2
.

Some nice extensions of these inequalities have been given by Bhatia-Holbrook
[
16
]

and Hirzallah-Kittaneh
[
69
]
. The next series of corollaries provide several eigenvalue inequalities completing those of Bhatia-Holbrook and Hirzallah-Kittaneh. First, we state the following improvement of (
3.4.1
).

Theorem 3.4.1
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and
p>2
. Then there exists two unitarie
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
such that

U\left|\frac{A+B}{2}\right|^{p}U^{*}+V\left|\frac{A-B}{2}\right|^{p}V^{*}\leq\frac{|A|^{p}+|B|^{p}}{2}.

Proof.

Note that

\left|\frac{A+B}{2}\right|^{p}=\left(\frac{|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}+A^{*}B+B^{*}A}{4}\right)^{p/2}

and

\left|\frac{A-B}{2}\right|^{p}=\left(\frac{|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}-(A^{*}B+B^{*}A)}{4}\right)^{p/2}.

Now, recall
Corollary
3.3.2
: Given two positive matrices
X,Y
and a monotone convex function
g(t
) defined on
[0,\infty)

such that
g(0)\leq 0
, we have

g(X+Y)\geq U_{0}g(X)U_{0}^{*}+V_{0}g(Y)V_{0}^{*}

(3.4.2)

for some pair of unitary matrices
U_{0}
and
V_{0}
. Applying this to
g(t)=t^{p/2}
,

X=\frac{|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}+A^{*}B+B^{*}A}{4}

and

Y=\frac{|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}-(A^{*}B+B^{*}A)}{4},

we obtain

\left(\frac{|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}}{2}\right)^{p/2}\geq U_{0}\left|\frac{A+B}{2}\right|^{p}U_{0}^{*}+V_{0}\left|\frac{A-B}{2}\right|^{p}V_{0}^{*}.

(3.4.3)

Next, recall Corollary
3.2.2
: Given two positive matrices
X,Y
and a monotone convex function
g(t
) defined on

[0,\infty)
, we have

\frac{g(X)+g(Y)}{2}\geq Wg\left(\frac{X+Y}{2}\right)W^{*}

(3.4.4)

for some unitary matrix
W
. Applying this to
g(t)=t^{p/2}
,
X=|A|^{2}
and
Y=|B|^{2}
, we get

\frac{|A|^{p}+|B|^{p}}{2}\geq W\left(\frac{|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}}{2}\right)^{p/2}W^{*}.

(3.4.5)

Combining (
3.4.3
) and (
3.4.5
) completes the proof with
U=WU_{0}
and
V=WV_{0}
.
∎

Corollary 3.4.2
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and
p>2
. Then, for all
k=1,2,\ldots,n
,

\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\uparrow}\left(\frac{|A|^{p}+|B|^{p}}{2}\right)\geq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\uparrow}\left(\left|\frac{A+B}{2}\right|^{p}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\uparrow}\left(\left|\frac{A-B}{2}\right|^{p}\right).

Corollary 3.4.3
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and
p>2
. Then, for all
k=1,2,\ldots,n
,

\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\uparrow}\left(\frac{|A|^{p}+|B|^{p}}{2}\right)\right\}^{1/k}\geq\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\uparrow}\left(\left|\frac{A+B}{2}\right|^{p}\right)\right\}^{1/k}+\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\uparrow}\left(\left|\frac{A-B}{2}\right|^{p}\right)\right\}^{1/k}.

Here
\lambda_{1}^{\uparrow}(X)\leq\lambda_{2}^{\uparrow}(X)\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{n}^{\uparrow}(X)
stand for the eigenvalues of

X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
arranged in the nondecresaing order.
These two corollaries follow from Theorem
3.4.1
and the fact that the functionals on
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}

X\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\uparrow}(X)

and

X\mapsto\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\uparrow}(X)\right\}^{1/k}

are two basic examples of symmetric anti-norms, see
[
28
]
,
[
29
]
.

The next corollary follows from Theorem
3.4.1
combined with a classical inequality of Weyl for the eigenvalues of the sum of two
Hermitian matrices.

Corollary 3.4.4
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and
p>2
. Then, for all
j,k\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
j+k+1\leq n
,

\lambda_{j+1}^{\downarrow}\left(\frac{|A|^{p}+|B|^{p}}{2}\right)\geq\lambda_{j+k+1}^{\downarrow}\left(\left|\frac{A+B}{2}\right|^{p}\right)+\lambda_{k+1}^{\uparrow}\left(\left|\frac{A-B}{2}\right|^{p}\right).

For a monotone concave function
g(t
) defined on
[0,\infty)
such that
g(0)\geq 0
, the inequalities (
3.4.2
) and (
3.4.4
)
are reversed.
Applying this to
g(t)=t^{q/2}
,
2>q>0
, the same proof than that of Theorem
3.4.1
gives the following statement.

Theorem 3.4.5
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and
2>q>0
. Then, for some unitaries
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

U\left|\frac{A+B}{2}\right|^{q}U^{*}+V\left|\frac{A-B}{2}\right|^{q}V^{*}\geq\frac{|A|^{q}+|B|^{q}}{2}.

By using Weyl’s inequality, this theorem yields an interesting eigenvalue estimate.

Corollary 3.4.6
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and
2>q>0
. Then, for all
j,k\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
j+k+1\leq n
,

\lambda_{j+k+1}^{\downarrow}\left(\frac{|A|^{q}+|B|^{q}}{2}\right)\leq\lambda_{j+1}^{\downarrow}\left(\left|\frac{A+B}{2}\right|^{q}\right)+\lambda_{k+1}^{\downarrow}\left(\left|\frac{A-B}{2}\right|^{q}\right).

Chapter 4
Around Hermite-Hadamard

Matrix inequalities and majorizations around Hermite-Hadamard’s inequality

[
43
]

Abstract.
We study the classical Hermite-Hadamard inequality in the matrix setting.
This leads to a number of interesting matrix inequalities such as the Schatten
p
-norm estimates

\left(\|A^{q}\|_{p}^{p}+\|B^{q}\|_{p}^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leq\|(xA+(1-x)B))^{q}\|_{p}+\|(1-x)A+xB)^{q}\|_{p}

for all positive (semidefinite)
n\times n
matrices
A,B
and
0<q,x<1
. A related decomposition, with the assumption
X^{*}X+Y^{*}Y=XX^{*}+YY^{*}=I
, is

(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY)\oplus(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{k=1}^{2n}U_{k}(A\oplus B)U_{k}^{*}

for some family of
2n\times 2n
unitary matrices
U_{k}
. This is a majorization which is obtained by using the Hansen-Pedersen trace inequality.

Keywords.
Positive definite matrices, block matrices, convex functions, matrix inequalities.

2010 mathematics subject classification.
15A18, 15A60, 47A30.

4.1
Elementary scalar inequalities

Extending basic scalar inequalities, for instance
|a+b|\leq|a|+|b|
, to matrices lies at the very heart of matrix analysis. Here, we are interested in the elementary inequality which supports the Hermite-Hadamard inequality.
This classical theorem can be stated as follows:

Proposition 4.1.1
.

Let
f(t)
be a convex function defined on the interval
[a,b]
. Then,

f\left(\frac{a+b}{2}\right)\ \leq\int_{0}^{1}f((1-x)a+xb)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq\frac{f(a)+f(b)}{2}.

In spite of its simplicity, the Hermite-Hadamard inequality is a powerful tool for deriving a number of important inequalities; see the nice paper
[
83
]
and references therein.

The first inequality immediately follows from the convexity assumption

f\left(\frac{a+b}{2}\right)\leq\frac{f((1-x)a+xb)+f(xa+(1-x)b)}{2}

(4.1.1)

The second inequality is slightly more subtle; it follows from the extremal property

f((1-x)a+xb)+f(xa+(1-x)b)\leq f(a)+f(b)

(4.1.2)

which requires the convexity assumption twice.
This is the key for Proposition
4.1.1
and it has a clear geometric interpretation; (
4.1.2
) is equivalent to the increasingness of

\varphi(t):=f(m+t)+f(m-t)

with
m=(a+b)/2
and
t\in[0,b-m]
. In fact, if we assume that
f(t)
is
C^{2}
and observe that for
t\in[0,b-m]
,

\varphi^{\prime}(t)=f^{\prime}(m+t)-f^{\prime}(m-t)=\int_{m-t}^{m+t}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s,

we can estimate
\varphi^{\prime}(t)
with
f^{\prime\prime}(s)\geq 0
.

The extremal property (
4.1.2
) of
f(t)
says that for four points in
[a,b]
,

p\leq s\leq t\leq q,\ p+q=s+t\Rightarrow f(s)+f(t)\leq f(p)+f(q).

(4.1.3)

Let us see now what can be said for matrices.
Important matrix versions of (
4.1.1
) are well-known. Let
\mathbb{M}_{n}
denote the space of
n\times n
matrices and
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
its self-adjoint (Hermitian) part with the usual order
\leq
induced by the positive semidefinite cone
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
. We recall
[
31
, Corollary 2.2]
.

Theorem 4.1.2
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
with spectra in
[a,b]
and let
f(t)
be a convex function on
[a,b]
. Then, for some unitaries
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

f\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\{U\frac{f(A)+f(B)}{2}U^{*}+V\frac{f(A)+f(B)}{2}V^{*}\right\}.

If furthermore
f(t)
is monotone, then we can take
U=V
.

Theorem
4.1.2
is a major improvement of the classical trace inequality of von Neumann (around 1920),

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\frac{f(A)+f(B)}{2}

which entails the following trivial extension of Proposition
4.1.1
.

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Let
f(t)
be a convex function defined on the interval
[a,b]
and let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
with spectra in
[a,b]
. Then,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)\ \leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\int_{0}^{1}f((1-x)A+xB)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\frac{f(A)+f(B)}{2}.

What about matrix versions of the equivalent scalar inequalities (
4.1.2
) and (
4.1.3
)? There is no hope for (
4.1.3
) : in general the trace inequality

{\mathrm{Tr}\,}f(P)+{\mathrm{Tr}\,}f(Q)\leq{\mathrm{Tr}\,}f(S)+{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f(T)

does not hold for all Hermitian matrices
P,Q,S,T
with spectra in
[a,b]
and such that

P\leq S\leq T\leq Q,\hskip 9.24994ptP+Q=S+T.

In the matrix setting (
4.1.2
) and (
4.1.3
) are not equivalent. This paper aims to establish two matrix versions of the extremal inequality (
4.1.2
). Doing so, we will obtain several new matrix inequalities.

For an operator convex functions
h(t)
on
[a,b]
, and
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
with spectra in this interval, the matrix version of (
4.1.2
) (as well as Proposition
4.1.1
) obvioulsy holds,

h((1-x)A+xB)+h(xA+(1-x)B)\leq h(A)+h(B)

(4.1.4)

for any
0<x<1
. Our results hold for much more general convex/concave functions and have applications to eigenvalue inequalities that cannot be derived from
(
4.1.4
), even in the case of the simplest operator convex/concave function
h(t)=t
.

We often use a crucial assumption: our functions are defined on the positive half-line and we deal with positive semidefinite matrices. In the matrix setting, the interval of definition of a function may be quite important; for instance the class of operator monotone functions on the whole real line reduces to affine functions.

4.2
The extremal property for matrices

For concave functions, the inequality (
4.1.2
) is reversed. Here is the matrix version. An isometry

U\in\mathbb{M}_{2n,n}
means a
2n\times n
matrix such that
U^{*}U=I
, the identity of
\mathbb{M}_{n}
.

Theorem 4.2.1
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, let
0<x<1
, and let
f(t)
be a monotone concave function on
[0,\infty)
with
f(0)\geq 0
. Then, for some isometry matrices
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{2n,n}
,

f(A)\oplus f(B)\leq Uf((1-x)A+xB)U^{*}+Vf(xA+(1-x)B)V^{*}.

Proof.

Let
x=\sin^{2}\theta
,
1-x=\cos^{2}\theta
, consider the unitary Hermitian matrix

R:=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{1-x}I&\sqrt{x}I\\
\sqrt{x}I&-\sqrt{1-x}I\end{bmatrix}

and note the unitary congruence

R\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}R=\begin{bmatrix}(1-x)A+xB&\star\\
\star&xA+(1-x)B\end{bmatrix}

(4.2.1)

where the stars hold for unspecified entries.

Now, recall the decomposition
[
31
, Lemma 3.4]
: Given any positive semidefinite matrix
\begin{bmatrix}C&X\\
X^{*}&D\end{bmatrix}
partitioned in four blocks in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
, we have

\begin{bmatrix}C&X\\
X^{*}&D\end{bmatrix}=U_{0}\begin{bmatrix}C&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}U_{0}^{*}+V_{0}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}V_{0}^{*}

for some unitary matrices
U_{0},V_{0}\in\mathbb{M}_{2n}
. Applying this to (
4.2.1
), we obtain

\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}=U_{1}\begin{bmatrix}(1-x)A+xB&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}U_{1}^{*}+V_{1}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&xA+(1-x)B\end{bmatrix}V_{1}^{*}

(4.2.2)

for two unitary matrices
U_{1},V_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{2n}
.

Next, recall the subadditivity inequality
[
31
, Theorem 3.4]
: Given any pair of positive semidefinite matrices
S,T\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
, we have

f(S+T)\leq U_{2}f(S)U_{2}^{*}+V_{2}f(T)V_{2}^{*}

for two unitary matrices
U_{2},V_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
. Applying this to (
4.2.2
) yields

\begin{bmatrix}f(A)&0\\
0&f(B)\end{bmatrix}\leq U\begin{bmatrix}f((1-x)A+xB)&0\\
0&f(0)I\end{bmatrix}U^{*}+V\begin{bmatrix}f(0)I&0\\
0&f(xA+(1-x)B)\end{bmatrix}V^{*}

for two unitary matrices
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{2n}
. This proves the theorem when
f(0)=0
.

To derive the general case, we may assume that
f(t)
is continuous (a concave function on
[0,\infty)
might be discontinuous at
0
). Indeed, it suffices to consider the values of
f(t)
on a finite set, the union of the spectra of the four matrices
A
,
B
,
(1-x)A+xB
and
xA+(1-x)B
. Hence we may replace
f(t)
by a piecewise affine monotone concave function
h(t)
with
h(0)\geq 0
. Now, since
h(t)
is continuous, a limit argument allows us to suppose that
A
and
B
are invertible. Therefore, letting
\lambda_{n}^{\downarrow}(Z)
denote the smallest eigenvalue of
Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
,

r:=\min\{\lambda_{n}^{\downarrow}(A),\lambda_{n}^{\downarrow}(B)\}>0.

We may then replace
h(t)
by
h_{r}(t)
defined as
h_{r}(t):=h(t)
for
t\geq r
,
h_{r}(0):=0
and
h_{r}(s):=h(r)\frac{s}{r}
for
0\leq s\leq r
. The function
h_{r}(t)
is monotone concave on
[0,\infty)
and vanishes at
0
, thus the case
f(0)=0
entails the general case.
∎

Let
\lambda_{j}^{\downarrow}(Z)
,
j=1,2,\ldots n
, denote the eigenvalues of
Z\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
arranged in the nonincreasing order.

Corollary 4.2.2
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, let
0<x<1
, and let
f(t)
be a nonnegative concave function on
[0,\infty)
. Then, for
j=0,1,\ldots,n-1
,

\lambda_{1+2j}^{\downarrow}\left(f(A\oplus B)\right)\leq\lambda_{1+j}^{\downarrow}\left(f(xA+(1-x)B)\right)+\lambda_{1+j}^{\downarrow}\left(f((1-x)A+xB)\right)

and

\lambda_{1+j}^{\downarrow}\left\{\left(f(xA+(1-x)B)\right)+\left(f((1-x)A+xB)\right)\right\}\leq 2\lambda_{1+j}^{\downarrow}\left(f\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)\right).

Proof.

The first inequality is a straighforward consequence of Theorem
4.2.1
combined with the inequalities of Weyl
[
13
, p. 62]
:
For all
S,T\in\mathbb{M}_{d}^{s.a}
and
j,k\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
such that
j+k+1\leq d
,

\lambda_{1+j+k}^{\downarrow}(S+T)\leq\lambda_{1+j}^{\downarrow}(S)+\lambda_{1+k}^{\downarrow}(T).

The second inequality is not new; it follows from Theorem
8.5.1
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.3
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, let
0<x<1
, and let
f(t)
be nonnegative concave function on
[0,\infty)
. Then, for all
p\geq 1
,

\left(\|f(A)\|_{p}^{p}+\|f(B)\|_{p}^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leq\|f(xA+(1-x)B))\|_{p}+\|f((1-x)A+xB)\|_{p}.

Proof.

From Theorem
4.2.1
we have

\|f(A)\oplus f(B)\|_{p}\leq\|Uf((1-x)A+xB)U^{*}+Vf(xA+(1-x)B)V^{*}\|_{p}.

The triangle inequality for
\|\cdot\|_{p}
completes the proof.
∎

Corollary
4.2.3
with
f(t)=t^{q}
reads as the following trace inequality.

Corollary 4.2.4
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
and
0<x<1
. Then, for all
p\geq 1\geq q\geq 0
,

\left\{{\mathrm{Tr\,}}A^{pq}+\mathrm{Tr\,}B^{pq}\right\}^{1/p}\leq\left\{{\mathrm{Tr\,}}(xA+(1-x)B)^{pq}\right\}^{1/p}+\left\{{\mathrm{Tr\,}}((1-x)A+xB))^{pq}\right\}^{1/p}.

Choosing in Corollary
4.2.4

q=1
and
x=1/2
yields McCarthy’s inequality,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}A^{p}+{\mathrm{Tr\,}}B^{p}\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}(A+B)^{p}.

This shows that Theorem
4.2.1
is already significant with
f(t)=t
.
Our next corollary, for convex functions, is equivalent to Theorem
4.2.1
.

Corollary 4.2.5
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, let
0<x<1
, and let
g(t)
be a monotone convex function on
[0,\infty)
with
g(0)\leq 0
. Then, for some isometry matrices
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{2n,n}
,

g(A)\oplus g(B)\geq Ug((1-x)A+xB)U^{*}+Vg(xA+(1-x)B)V^{*}.

Since the Schatten
q
-quasinorms
\|\cdot\|_{q}
,
0<q<1
, are superadditive functionals on
\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, Corollary
4.2.5
yields the next one.

Corollary 4.2.6
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, let
0<x<1
, and let
g(t)
be a nonnegative convex function on
[0,\infty)
with
g(0)\leq 0
. Then, for all
0<q<1
,

\left(\|g(A)\|_{q}^{q}+\|g(B)\|_{q}^{q}\right)^{1/q}\geq\|g(xA+(1-x)B))\|_{q}+\|g((1-x)A+xB)\|_{q}.

From the first inequality of Corollary
4.2.2
we also get the following statement.

Corollary 4.2.7
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
and let
f(t)
be a nonnegative concave function on
[0,\infty)
. Then, for
j=0,1,\ldots,n-1
,

\lambda_{1+2j}^{\downarrow}\left(f(A\oplus B)\right)\leq 2\int_{0}^{1}\lambda_{1+j}^{\downarrow}\left(f(xA+(1-x)B)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

Up to now we have dealt with convex combinations
(1-x)A+xB
with scalar weights. It is natural to search for extensions with matricial weights (
C^{*}
-convex combinations). We may
generalize Theorem
4.2.1
with commuting normal weights.

Theorem 4.2.8
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
and let
f(t)
be a monotone concave function on
[0,\infty)
with
f(0)\geq 0
. If
X,Y\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
are normal and satisfy
XY=YX
and
X^{*}X+Y^{*}Y=I
, then, for some isometry matrices
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{2n,n}
,

f(A)\oplus f(B)\leq Uf(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY)U^{*}+Vf(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)V^{*}.

Proof.

The proof is quite similar to that of Theorem
4.2.1
except that we first observe that the
2n\times 2n
matrix

H:=\begin{bmatrix}X&Y\\
Y&-X\end{bmatrix}

is unitary. Indeed for two normal operators,
XY=YX
ensures
X^{*}Y=YX^{*}
and a direct computation shows that
H^{*}H
is the identity in
\mathbb{M}_{2n}
.
We then use the unitary congruence

H^{*}\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}H=\begin{bmatrix}X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY&\star\\
\star&Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX\end{bmatrix}

(4.2.3)

where the stars hold for unspecified entries.
∎

Hence, in the first inequality of Corolloray
4.2.2
and in the series of Corollaries
4.2.3
-
4.2.6
, we can replace the scalar convex combinations
(1-x)A+B
and
xA+(1-x)B
by
C^{*}
-convex combinations
X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY
and
Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX
with commuting normal weights. Here we explicitly state the generalization of Corollary
4.2.5
.

Corollary 4.2.9
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
and let
g(t)
be a monotone convex function on
[0,\infty)
with
g(0)\leq 0
. If
X,Y\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
are normal and satisfy
XY=YX
and
X^{*}X+Y^{*}Y=I
, then, for some isometry matrices
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{2n,n}
,

g(A)\oplus g(B)\geq Ug(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY)U^{*}+Vg(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)V^{*}.

4.3
Majorization

The results of Section 2 require two essential assumptions : to deal with positive matrices and with subadditive (concave) or superadditive (convex) functions. Thanks to these assumptions, we have obtained operator inequalities for the usual order in the positive cone.

The results of this section will consider Hermitian matrices and general convex or concave functions. We will obtain majorization relations. We also consider
C^{*}
-convex combinations more general than those with commuting normal weights.

We recall the notion of majorization. Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
.
We say that
A
is weakly majorized by
B
and we write
A\prec_{w}B
, if

\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\downarrow}(A)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\downarrow}(B)

for all
k=1,2,\ldots n
. If
furthemore the equality holds for
k=n
, that is
A
and
B
have the same trace, then we say that
A
is majorized by
B
, written
A\prec B
. See
[
13
, Chapter 2]
and
[
64
]
for a background on majorization. One easily checks that
A\prec_{w}B
is equivalent to
A+C\prec B
for some
C\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
. We need two fundamental principles:

(1)

A\prec B\Rightarrow g(A)\prec_{w}g(B)
for all convex functions
g(t)
.

(2)

A\prec_{w}B\iff{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f(A)\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}f(B)
for all nondecreasing convex functions
f(t)
. Equivalently
A\prec B\iff{\mathrm{Tr\,}}g(A)\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}g(B)
for all convex functions
g(t)
.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
and let
g(t)
be a convex function defined on an interval containing the spectra of
A
and
B
. If
X,Y\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
satisfy
X^{*}X+Y^{*}Y=XX^{*}+YY^{*}=I
, then,

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\left\{g(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY)+g(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)\right\}\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\left\{g(A)+g(B)\right\}.

Proof.

By the famous Hansen-Pedersen trace inequality
[
67
]
, see also
[
31
, Corollary 2.4]
for a generalization,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr\,}}

\displaystyle\left\{g(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY)+g(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}\left\{X^{*}g(A)X+Y^{*}g(B)Y)+Y^{*}g(A)Y+X^{*}g(B)X\right\}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr\,}}\left\{(g(A)+g(B))(XX^{*}+YY^{*})\right\}={\mathrm{Tr\,}}\left\{g(A)+g(B)\right\}

where the first equality follows from the cyclicity of the trace.
∎

Theorem 4.3.2
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
and
X,Y\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. If
X^{*}X+Y^{*}Y=XX^{*}+YY^{*}=I
, then, for some unitary matrices
\{U_{k}\}_{k=1}^{2n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{2n}
,

(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY)\oplus(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{k=1}^{2n}U_{k}(A\oplus B)U_{k}^{*}.

Proof.

From Lemma
4.3.1
, we have the trace inequality

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}g\left((X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY)\oplus(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)\right)\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}g(A\oplus B)

for all convex functions defined on
(-\infty,\infty)
. By a basic principle of majorization, this is equivalent to

(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY)\oplus(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)\prec A\oplus B.

(4.3.1)

By
[
42
, Proposition 2.6]
, the majorization in
\mathbb{M}_{d}^{s.a}
,
S\prec T
, ensures that (and thus is equivalent to)

S\leq\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}V_{j}TV_{j}^{*}

for
d
unitary matrices
V_{j}\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
. Applying this to (
4.3.1
) completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.3.3
.

We can prove the majorization (
4.3.1
) in a different way by oberving that our assumption on
X
and
Y
ensures that the map
\Phi
, defined on
\mathbb{M}_{d}
, (here
d=2n
),

\Phi\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&C\\
D&B\end{bmatrix}\right):=\begin{bmatrix}X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY&0\\
0&Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)\end{bmatrix},

is a positive linear map unital and trace preserving. Such maps are also called doubly stochastic. It is a classical result (see Ando’s survey
[
5
, Section 7]
and references therein) that we have

\Phi(Z)\prec Z

(4.3.2)

for every doubly stochastic map on
\mathbb{M}_{d}
and Hermitian
Z
. Here, our map is even completely positive (it is a so called quantum channel). It is well known in the litterature (
[
5
, Theorem 7.1]
) that we have then

\Phi(Z)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}t_{j}U_{j}^{*}ZU_{j}

for some convex combination
0<t_{j}\leq 1
,
\sum_{j=1}^{m}t_{j}=1
, and some unitary matrices
U_{j}
(these scalars
t_{i}
and matrices
U_{i}
depend on
Z
). In 2003, Zhan
[
94
]
noted that we can take
m=d
. That we can actually take an average,

\Phi(Z)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}U_{j}^{*}ZU_{j},

follows from the quite recent observation
[
42
, Proposition 2.6]
mentioned in the proof of Theorem
4.3.2
. For quantum channels, one has the Choi-Kraus decomposition (see
[
15
, Chapter 3]
)

\Phi(A)=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{2}}K_{i}AK_{i}^{*}

for some weights
K_{i}\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{d^{2}}K_{i}K_{i}^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{2}}K_{i}^{*}K_{i}=I
. So, the proof of
Lemma 3.1 shows that, for quantum channels, one may derive the fundamental majorization (
4.3.2
) from two results for convex functions: the basic principle of majorisation and Hansen-Pedersen’s trace inequality.

Theorem
4.3.2
combined with Theorem
4.1.2
provide a number of interesting operator inequalities. The next corollary can be regarded as another matrix version of the scalar inequality (
4.1.2
). We will give a proof independent of Theorem
4.1.2
.

Corollary 4.3.4
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{s.a}
and let
f(t)
be a convex function defined on an interval containing the spectra of
A
and
B
.
If
X,Y\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
satisfy
X^{*}X+Y^{*}Y=XX^{*}+YY^{*}=I
, then, for some unitary matrices
\{U_{k}\}_{k=1}^{2n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{2n}
,

f\left(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY\right)\oplus f\left(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX\right)\leq\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{k=1}^{2n}U_{k}f\left(A\oplus B\right)U_{k}^{*}.

In particular, for the absolute value, we note that

\left|X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY\right|\oplus\left|Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX\right|\leq\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{k=1}^{4n}U_{k}\left|A\oplus B\right|U_{k}^{*}.

Proof.

The majorization (
4.3.1
) and the basic principle of majorizations show
that

f\left(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY\right)\oplus f\left(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX\right)\prec_{w}f(A\oplus B)

for all convex functions defined on an interval containing the spectra of
A
and
B
.
Thus

f\left(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY\right)\oplus f\left(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX\right)+C\prec f(A\oplus B)

for some positive semidefinte matrice
C\in\mathbb{M}_{2n}^{+}
. Hence, as in the previous proof,

f\left(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY\right)\oplus f\left(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX\right)+C\leq\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{k=1}^{2n}U_{k}f\left(A\oplus B\right)U_{k}^{*}

for some family of unitary matrices
U_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{2n}
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.5
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
. If
X,Y\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
satisfy
X^{*}X+Y^{*}Y=XX^{*}+YY^{*}=I
, then,

\det(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY)\det(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX)\geq\det A\det B.

Proof.

Since the classical Minkowski functional
Z\mapsto\det^{1/2n}Z
is concave on
\mathbb{M}_{2n}^{+}
, the result is an immediate consequence of Theorem
4.3.2
.
∎

The case
A=B
and
X,Y
are two orthogonal projections reads as the classical Fisher’s inequality.

Corollary
4.3.4
yields inequalities for symmetric norms and antinorms on
\mathbb{M}_{2n}^{+}
.
Symmetric norms,
\|\cdot\|
, also called unitarily invariant norms, are classical objects in matrix analysis. We refer to
[
13
]
,
[
64
]
,
[
67
, Chapter 6]
and, in the setting of compact operators,
[
89
]
. The most famous examples are the Schatten
p
-norms,
1\leq p\leq\infty
, and the Ky Fan
k
-norms.

Symmetric anti-norms
\|\cdot\|_{!}
are the concave counterpart of symmetric norms. Famous examples are the Schatten
q
-quasi norms,
0<q<1
and the Minkowski functional considered in the proof of Corollary
4.3.5
. We refer to
[
28
]
and
[
29
, Section 4]
for much more examples.

Corollary 4.3.6
.

Let
f(t)
and
g(t)
be two nonnegative functions defined on
[a,b]
and let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
be Hermitian with spectra in
[a,b]
. If
X,Y\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
satisfy
X^{*}X+Y^{*}Y=XX^{*}+YY^{*}=I
, then :

(i)

If
f(t)
is concave, then, for all symmetric antinorms,

\left\|f\left(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY\right)\oplus f\left(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX\right)\right\|_{!}\geq\left\|f\left(A\oplus B\right)\right\|_{!}.

(ii)

If
g(t)
is convex, then, for all symmetric norms,

\left\|g\left(X^{*}AX+Y^{*}BY\right)\oplus g\left(Y^{*}AY+X^{*}BX\right)\right\|\leq\left\|g\left(A\oplus B\right)\right\|.

Proof.

Since symmetric antinorms are unitarily invariant and superadditive, the first assertion follows from the version of Corollary
4.3.4
for concave version. The second assertion is an immediate consequence of Corollary
4.3.4
. ∎

We close this section by mentioning Moslehian’s weak majorization which provides a matrix version of the first inequality of Proposition
4.1.1
. We may restate
[
81
, Corollary 3.4]

as inequalities
for symmetric and antisymmetric norms.

Proposition 4.3.7
.

Let
f(t)
and
g(t)
be two nonnegative functions defined on
[a,b]
and let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
be Hermitian with spectra in
[a,b]
.

(i)

If
f(t)
is concave, then, for all symmetric antinorms,

\left\|f\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)\right\|_{!}\geq\left\|\int_{0}^{1}f((1-x)A+xB)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right\|_{!}.

(ii)

If
g(t)
is convex, then, for all symmetric norms,

\left\|g\left(\frac{A+B}{2}\right)\right\|\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{1}g((1-x)A+xB)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right\|.

4.4
