Chapter 7
Block matrices and numerical range

For sake of completeness the proof of Theorem
7.1.1
is given in Section 7.4 “Around this article”.

Eigenvalue inequalities for positive block matrices with the inradius of the numerical range

[
44
]

Abstract.
We prove the operator norm inequality, for a positive matrix partitioned into four blocks in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\|A+B\|_{\infty}+\delta(X),

where
\delta(X)
is the diameter of the largest possible disc in the numerical range of
X
. This shows that
the inradius
\varepsilon(X):=\delta(X)/2
satisfies

\varepsilon(X)\geq\|X\|_{\infty}-\|(|X^{*}|+|X|)/2\|_{\infty}.

Several eigenvalue inequalities are derived. In particular, if
X
is a normal matrix whose spectrum lies in a disc of radius
r
, the third eigenvalue of the full matrix is bounded by the second eigenvalue of the sum of the diagonal block,

\lambda_{3}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)\leq\lambda_{2}(A+B)+r.

We think that
r
is optimal and we propose a conjecture related to a norm inequality of Hayashi.

Keywords.
Numerical range, Partitioned matrices, eigenvalue inequalities.

2010 mathematics subject classification.
15A60, 47A12, 15A42, 47A30.

7.1
Introduction

Positive matrices partitioned into four blocks play a central role in Matrix Analysis, and in applications, for instance quantum information theory. A lot of
important theorems deal with these matrices. Some of these results give comparison between the full matrix and its diagonal blocks, in particular the sum of the diagonal blocks (the partial trace in the quantum terminology). This note focuses on a recent result of Bourin and Mhana
[
47
]
, involving the numerical range of the offdiagonal block. Recall that a symmetric norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{M}_{2n}
means a unitarily invariant norm. It induces a symmetric norm on
\mathbb{M}_{n}
in an obvious way. The Schatten
p
-norms
\|\cdot\|_{p}
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
, and the operator norm
(p=\infty)
are classical examples of symmetric norms. The main result of
[
47
]
reads as follows.

Theorem 7.1.1
.

Let
\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}
be a positive matrix partitioned into four blocks
in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Suppose that
W(X)
has the width
\omega
. Then, for all symmetric norms,

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|\leq\|A+B+\omega I\|.

Here
I
stands for the identity matrix,
W(X)
denotes the numerical range of
X
, and the width of
W(X)
is the smallest distance between two parallel straight lines such that the strip between these two lines contains
W(X)
. If
\omega=0
, that is
W(X)
is a line segment, Theorem 1.1 was first proved by Mhanna
[
80
]
. Recently
[
41
]
, Theorem
7.1.1
has been completed with the reversed inequality

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|\geq\left\|\begin{bmatrix}\frac{A+B}{2}+dI&0\\
0&\frac{A+B}{2}-dI\end{bmatrix}\right\|.

where
d:=\min\{|z|\,:\,z\in W(X)\}
is the distance from
0
to
W(X)
. Several applications
were derived.

Some equality cases in Theorem
7.1.1
occur for the operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
with the following block matrices, where
a,b
are two arbitrary nonnegative real numbers.

\begin{bmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&b\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\
b&0\end{pmatrix}\\
\begin{pmatrix}0&b\\
a&0\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}b&0\\
0&a\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}.

This follows from the fact that

W\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&b\\
a&0\end{pmatrix}\right)

has the width
2\left||a|-|b|\right|
.

Though Theorem
7.1.1
is sharp for the operator norm, a subtle improvement is possible. This is our concern in the next section.
Once again, a geometric feature of
W(X)
will contribute: its inradius. Our approach leads to a remarkable list of eigenvalue that cannot be derived from the norm inequalities of Theorem
7.1.1
.
The last section is devoted to some related operator norm inequalities, in particular we will discuss a property due to Hayashi (2019) and propose a conjecture.

7.2
Eigenvalue inequalities

We define the indiameter
\delta(\Lambda)
of a compact convex set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{C}
as the diameter of the largest possible disc in
\Lambda
. For matrices
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, we shorten

\delta(W(X))=:\delta(X)
. Recall that the numerical range of a two-by-two matrix is an elliptical disc (or a line segment, or a single point).

A matrix
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
is identified as an operator on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. If
{\mathcal{S}}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we denote by
X_{\mathcal{S}}
the compression of
X
onto
{\mathcal{S}}
.
We then define the
elliptical width
of
X
as

\delta_{2}(X):=\sup_{\dim{\mathcal{S}}=2}\delta(X_{\mathcal{S}}).

Of course
\delta_{2}(X)\leq\delta(X)\leq\omega
where
\omega
still denotes the width of
W(X)
. If
X
is a contraction, then
\delta_{2}(X)\leq 1
, while
\delta(X)
may be arbitrarily close to 2 (letting
n
be large enough). We state our main result.

Theorem 7.2.1
.

Let
\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}
be a positive matrix partitioned into four blocks
in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Then, for all
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\}
,

\lambda_{1+2j}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)\leq\lambda_{1+j}(A+B)+\delta_{2}(X).

Here
\lambda_{1}(S)\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{d}(S)
stand for the eigenvalue of any Hermitian matrix
S\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
. If we denote by
\lambda_{1}^{\uparrow}(S)\leq\cdots\leq\lambda^{\uparrow}_{d}(S)
these eigenvalues arranged in the increasing order, then Theorem
7.2.1
reads as

\lambda_{2k}^{\uparrow}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)\leq\lambda_{k}^{\uparrow}(A+B)+\delta_{2}(X).

for all
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.

The case
j=0
in Theorem
7.2.1
improves Theorem
7.1.1
for the operator norm. We may consider that Theorem
7.2.1
is trivial for
j=n-1
. Indeed, using the decomposition
[
31
, Lemma 3.4]
,

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}=U\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}U^{*}+V\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}V^{*},

for some unitary matrices
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{2n}
, we obtain from Weyl’s inequality

[
13
, p. 62]
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{2n-1}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{n}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\right)+\lambda_{n}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n}(A)+\lambda_{n}(B)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{n}(A+B).

We turn to the proof of the theorem.

Proof.

We first consider the case
j=0
. We may assume that the norm of the block matrix is strictly greater than
the norms of its two diagonal blocks
A
and
B
, otherwise the statement is trivial. Hence we have two nonzero (column) vectors
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that
\|h_{1}\|^{2}+\|h_{2}\|^{2}=1
and

\lambda_{1}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)=\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}=\begin{pmatrix}h^{*}_{1}&h_{2}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore, denoting by
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
the rank one projections corresponding to the one dimensional subspaces spanned by
h_{1}
and by
h_{2}
, we have

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}=\left\|\begin{bmatrix}E_{1}&0\\
0&E_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}E_{1}&0\\
0&E_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}

Hence, denoting by
F
a rank two projection such that
E_{1}\leq F
and
E_{2}\leq F
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|\begin{bmatrix}F&0\\
0&F\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}F&0\\
0&F\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|\begin{bmatrix}FAF&FXF\\
FX^{*}F&FBF\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}.

So, letting
{\mathcal{S}}
denote the range of
F
, we have

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}=\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A_{\mathcal{S}}&X_{\mathcal{S}}\\
X^{*}_{\mathcal{S}}&B_{\mathcal{S}}\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}.

Hence applying Theorem
7.1.1
for the operator norm, we obtain

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\|A_{\mathcal{S}}+B_{\mathcal{S}}\|_{\infty}+\varepsilon

where
\varepsilon
is the width of
W(X_{\mathcal{S}})
. Since
W(X_{\mathcal{S}})
is an elliptical disc (as
X_{\mathcal{S}}
acts on a two-dimensional space), its width equals to its indiameter, hence
\varepsilon\leq\delta_{2}(X)
, and since

\|A_{\mathcal{S}}+B_{\mathcal{S}}\|_{\infty}=\|(A+B)_{\mathcal{S}}\|_{\infty}\leq\|A+B\|_{\infty}=\lambda_{1}(A+B),

the proof for
j=0
is complete.

We turn to the general case,
j=1,\ldots,n-1
. By the min-max principle,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1+2j}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\dim{\mathcal{S}}=n-j}\lambda_{1}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}_{{\mathcal{S}}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\dim{\mathcal{S}}=n-j}\lambda_{1}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A_{\mathcal{S}}&X_{\mathcal{S}}\\
X^{*}_{\mathcal{S}}&B_{\mathcal{S}}\end{bmatrix}\right),

hence, from the first part of the proof,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1+2j}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\dim{\mathcal{S}}=n-j}\lambda_{1}\left(A_{\mathcal{S}}+B_{\mathcal{S}}\right)+\delta_{2}(X)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1+j}(A+B)+\delta_{2}(X)

which is the desired claim. ∎

If
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, we denote by
{\mathrm{dist}}(X,\mathbb{C}I)
the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
-distance from
X
to
\mathbb{C}I
.
Thus, for a scalar perturbation of a contraction,
X=\lambda I+C
for some contraction
C\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, we have
{\mathrm{dist}}(X,\mathbb{C}I)\leq 1
.

Corollary 7.2.2
.

Let
\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}
be a positive matrix partitioned into four blocks
in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Then, for all
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\}
,

\lambda_{1+2j}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)\leq\lambda_{1+j}(A+B)+{\mathrm{dist}}(X,\mathbb{C}I).

Proof.

For any subspace
{\mathcal{S}}\subset C^{n}
, we have

{\mathrm{dist}}(X,\mathbb{C}I)\geq{\mathrm{dist}}(X_{\mathcal{S}},\mathbb{C}I_{\mathcal{S}}).

If
{\mathcal{S}}
has dimension 2, then

{\mathrm{dist}}(X_{\mathcal{S}},\mathbb{C}I_{\mathcal{S}})\geq\delta\left(W(X_{\mathcal{S}})\right).

Therefore
{\mathrm{dist}}(X,\mathbb{C}I)\geq\delta_{2}(X)
and Theorem
7.2.1
completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 7.2.3
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Then, for every
j\geq 0
such that
1+2j\leq n
,

\lambda_{1+2j}\left(A^{*}A+B^{*}B\right)\leq\lambda_{1+j}\left(AA^{*}+BB^{*}\right)+\delta_{2}(AB^{*})

Proof.

Note that

\lambda_{1+2j}\left(A^{*}A+B^{*}B\right)=\lambda_{1+2j}\left(T^{*}T\right)=\lambda_{1+2j}\left(TT^{*}\right)

with
T=\begin{bmatrix}A\\
B\end{bmatrix}
and

TT^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}AA^{*}&AB^{*}\\
BA^{*}&BB^{*}\end{bmatrix}

so that Theorem
7.2.1
yields the desired claim.
∎

Corollary 7.2.4
.

Let
\begin{bmatrix}A&N\\
N^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}
be a positive matrix partitioned into four blocks
in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. If
N
is normal and its spectrum is contained in a disc of radius
r
, then,

\lambda_{1+2j}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&N\\
N^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)\leq\lambda_{1+j}(A+B)+r.

for all
j=0,1,\ldots,n-1
.

Proof.

Corollary
7.2.4
is a special case of corollary
7.2.2
, as
N=\lambda I+R
,
where
\lambda
is the center of the disc of radius
r
containing the spectrum of
N
, and
\|N\|_{\infty}\leq r
.
∎

Question 7.2.5
.

Fix
r>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. Can we find (with
n
large enough) a normal matrix
N
with spectrum in a disc of radius
r
and a positive block matrix
\begin{bmatrix}A&N\\
N^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}

such that

\lambda_{1+2j}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A&N\\
N^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right)\geq\lambda_{1+j}(A+B)+r-\varepsilon

for some
j\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
? Is it true for for
j=0
?

7.3
Norm inequalities

Corollary
7.2.4
with
j=0
reads as follows.

Corollary 7.3.1
.

Let
\begin{bmatrix}A&N\\
N^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}
be a positive matrix partitioned into four blocks
in
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. If
N
is normal and its spectrum is contained in a disc of radius
r
, then,

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&N\\
N^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\|A+B\|_{\infty}+r.

We do not know wether the constant
r
is sharp or not (Question
7.2.5
). If
n=2
, we can replace
r
by
0
as the numerical range of
N
is then a line segment. If
n=3
there are some simple examples with
N=U
unitary such that

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&U\\
U^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}>\|A+B\|_{\infty}.

See Hayashi’s example in the discussion of [H, Problem 3] and the interesting study and examples in [G] where we further have
A+B=kI
for some scalars
k
. The next result is due to Hayashi [H, Theorem 2.5].

Theorem 7.3.2
.

Suppose that
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
is invertible with
n
distinct singular values. If
the inequality

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\|A+B\|_{\infty}.

holds for all positive block-matrix with
X
as off-diagonal block, then
X
is normal.

Theorem
7.3.2
and Theorem
7.1.1
suggest a natural conjecture. If
W(T)
is line segment, then
T
is a so-called essentially Hermitian matrix.

Conjecture 7.3.3
.

Let
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. If
the inequality

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\|A+B\|_{\infty}

holds for all positive block-matrix with
X
as off-diagonal block, then
X
is essentially Hermitian.

If we replace the operator norm by the Frobenius (or Hilbert-Schmidt) norm
\|\cdot\|_{2}
then the following characterization holds.

Proposition 7.3.4
.

Let
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Then,
the inequality

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}\leq\|A+B\|_{2}

holds for all positive block-matrix with
X
as off-diagonal block if and only if
X
is normal.

Proof.

Suppose that
X
is normal. To prove the inequality, squaring both side, it suffices to establish the trace inequality

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}X^{*}X\leq{\mathrm{Tr\,}}AB.

(7.3.1)

Note that
X=A^{1/2}KB^{1/2}
, for some contraction
K
. Recall that, for all symmetric norms on
\mathbb{M}_{n},
and any normal matrix
N\mathbb{M}_{n}
, decomposed as
N=ST
, we have

\|N\|\leq\|TS\|
. Therefore

\|X\|=\|A^{1/2}KB^{1/2}\|\leq\|KB^{1/2}A^{1/2}\|.

Squaring this inequality with the Frobenius norm yields the desired inequality (
7.3.1
).

Suppose that
X
is nonnormal, and note
that

\begin{bmatrix}|X^{*}|&X\\
X^{*}&|X|\end{bmatrix}
is positive semidefinite and satisfies

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}|X^{*}|&X\\
X^{*}&|X|\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}^{2}=4\||X|\|_{2}^{2}

while

\||X^{*}|+|X|\|_{2}^{2}=2\||X|\|_{2}^{2}+2{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|X||X^{*}|

In the Hilbert space
(\mathbb{M}_{n},\|\cdot\|_{2})
, the assumption

\||X|\|_{2}=\||X^{*}|\|_{2},\hskip 9.24994pt|X|\neq|X^{*}|

ensures strict inequality in the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|X||X^{*}|<\|X\|_{2}^{2}.

Therefore

\||X^{*}|+|X|\|_{2}^{2}<\left\|\begin{bmatrix}|X^{*}|&X\\
X^{*}&|X|\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}^{2}

and this completes the proof.
∎

Proposition
7.3.4
suggests a question: for which
p\in[1,\infty]
, the schatten
p
-norm inequality

\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&N\\
N^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{p}\leq\|A+B\|_{p}

holds for any positive partitioned matrices with a normal off-diagonal block
N
?

Corollary 7.3.5
.

Let
H,K,X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
be Hermitian. If
X
is invertible and
HK
is a scalar perturbation of a contraction, then,

\left\|XH^{2}X+X^{-1}K^{2}X^{-1}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|HX^{2}H+KX^{-2}K\right\|_{\infty}+1.

Proof.

We apply Corollary
7.2.3
with
j=0
and
A=HX
,
B=KX^{-1}
, to get

\left\|XH^{2}X+X^{-1}K^{2}X^{-1}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|HX^{2}H+KX^{-2}K\right\|_{\infty}+\delta_{2}(HK)

Since
(HK)_{\mathcal{S}}
is a scalar perturbation of a contraction acting on a space of dimension 2, necessarily
\delta_{2}(HK)\leq 1
.
∎

For a normal operator, the numerical range is the convex hull of the spectrum. For a non normal operator
X
, several lower bounds for the indiameter
of
W(X)
can be obtained from the left and right modulus
|X^{*}|
and
|X|
.

Corollary 7.3.6
.

Let
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and let
f(t)
and
g(t)
are two nonnegative functions defined on
[0,\infty)
such that
f(t)g(t)=t^{2}
. Then,

\delta_{2}(X)\geq\left\|f(|X|)+g(|X|)\right\|_{\infty}-\left\|f(|X^{*}|)+g(|X|)\right\|_{\infty}.

Proof.

First, observe that we have a function
h(t)
defined on
[0,\infty)
such that

f(t)=th^{2}(t^{1/2}),\hskip 9.24994ptg(t)=th^{-2}(t^{1/2}),

(7.3.2)

and
h(t)>0
for all
t\geq 0
(we may, for instance, set
h(0)=1
). Hence
h(T)
is invertible for any positive
T
, and from the polar decomposition

X=|X^{*}|^{1/2}U|X|^{1/2}

with a unitary factor
U
, we infer the factorization

X=|X^{*}|^{1/2}h(|X^{*}|^{1/2})U|X|^{1/2}h^{-1}(|X|^{1/2}).

Thus
X=AB^{*}
where

A=|X^{*}|^{1/2}h(|X^{*}|^{1/2})
and
B^{*}=U|X|^{1/2}h^{-1}(|X|^{1/2})
.
Therefore Corollary
7.2.3
yields

\left\||X^{*}|h^{2}(|X^{*}|^{1/2})+U|X|h^{-2}(|X|^{1/2})U^{*}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\||X|h^{2}(|X|^{1/2})+|X|h^{-2}(|X|^{1/2})U^{*}\right\|_{\infty}+\delta_{2}(X)

Using (
7.3.2
) and the fact that
\varphi(|X^{*}|)=U\varphi(|X|)U^{*}
for any function
\varphi(t)
defined on
[0,\infty)
, the proof is complete.
∎

The following special case shows that Corollary
7.3.6
is rather optimal.

Corollary 7.3.7
.

If
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
has a numerical range of inradius
\varepsilon(X)
, then, for all
a\in\mathbb{C}
,

\varepsilon(X)\geq\left\|X-aI\right\|_{\infty}-\left\|\frac{|X-aI|+|X^{*}-\overline{a}I|}{2}\right\|_{\infty}.

If
X\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
and
a=\tau
is the normalized trace of
X
, then this inequality is an equality.

Proof.

Applying Corollary
7.3.6
with
X-aI
and
f(t)=g(t)=t
yields the inequality. If
X\in\mathbb{M}_{2}
, then
X
is unitarily equivalent to

\begin{pmatrix}\tau&y\\
x&\tau\end{pmatrix}.

So

\displaystyle\|X-\tau I\|_{\infty}-\left\|\frac{|X-\tau I|+|X^{*}-\overline{\tau}I|}{2}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|\begin{pmatrix}|x|&0\\
0&|y|\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\begin{pmatrix}|x|+|y|&0\\
0&|x|+|y|\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left||x|-|y|\right|

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(X)

establishing the desired equality.
∎

The special case
f(t)=g(t)=t
in Corollary
7.3.6
seems important, we record it as a proposition:

Proposition 7.3.8
.

The elliptical width of the numerical range of
X\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
satisfies

\delta_{2}(X)\geq 2\|X\|_{\infty}-\||X|+|X^{*}|\|_{\infty}.

In particular, the inradius
\varepsilon(X)
of the numerical range of
X
satisfies

\varepsilon(X)\geq\|X\|_{\infty}-\|(|X|+|X^{*}|)/2\|_{\infty}.

Remark 7.3.9
.

Our results still hold for operators on infinite dimensional separable Hilbert space (assuming in Corollary
7.3.6
that
f(t)
and

g(t)
are Borel functions).

7.4
Around this article

We give the proof of Theorem
7.1.1
.

Proof.

(Theorem
7.1.1
) By using the unitary congruence implemented by

\begin{bmatrix}e^{i\theta}&0\\
0&I\end{bmatrix}

we see that our block matrix is unitarily equivalent to

\begin{bmatrix}A&e^{i\theta}X\\
e^{-i\theta}X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}.

As
W(e^{i\theta}X)=e^{i\theta}W(X)
, by choosing the adequate
\theta
and replacing
X
by
e^{i\theta}X
, we may and do assume that
W(X)
lies in a strip
{\mathcal{S}}

of width
\omega
and parallel to the imaginary axis,

{\mathcal{S}}=\left\{\,x+iy\ :\hskip 9.24994pty\in\mathbb{R},\hskip 9.24994ptr\leq x\leq r+\omega\,\right\}.

The projection property for the real part
{\mathrm{Re}\,}W(X)=W({\mathrm{Re}\,}X)
, then ensures that

rI\leq{\mathrm{Re}\,}X\leq(r+\omega)I.

(7.4.1)

Now we use the decomposition
[
45
, Corollary 2.1]
derived from (
8.2.1
),

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}=U\begin{bmatrix}\frac{A+B}{2}+{\mathrm{Re}\,}X&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}U^{*}+V\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&\frac{A+B}{2}-{\mathrm{Re}\,}X\end{bmatrix}V^{*}

(7.4.2)

for some unitaries
U,\,V\in\mathbb{M}_{2n}
. Note that the two matrices in the right hand side of (
7.4.2
) are positive since so are

\begin{bmatrix}I&I\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}I\\
I\end{bmatrix}\hskip 9.24994pt{\mathrm{and}}\begin{bmatrix}I&-I\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}I\\
-I\end{bmatrix}.

Combining (
7.4.1
) and (
7.4.2
) yields

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\leq U\begin{bmatrix}\frac{A+B}{2}+(r+\omega)I&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}U^{*}+V\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&\frac{A+B}{2}-rI\end{bmatrix}V^{*}

where the two matrices of the right hand side are positive. From each Ky Fan
k
-norm,
k=1,2,\ldots,2n
, we then have

\displaystyle\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{(k)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{A+B}{2}+(r+\omega)I\right\|_{(k)}+\left\|\frac{A+B}{2}-rI\right\|_{(k)}

\displaystyle=\left\|A+B+\omega I\right\|_{(k)}.

The Ky Fan principle then guarantees that this inequality hold for all symmetric norms.
∎

Chapter 8
Block matrices and Pythagoras

A Pythagorean theorem for partitioned matrices

[
42
]

Abstract.
We establish a Pythagorean theorem for the absolute values of the blocks of a partitioned matrix. This leads to a
series of remarkable operator inequalities. For instance, if the matrix
\mathbb{A}
is partitioned into three blocks
A,B,C
, then

|\mathbb{A}|^{3}\geq U|A|^{3}U^{*}+V|B|^{3}V^{*}+W|C|^{3}W^{*},

\sqrt{3}|\mathbb{A}|\geq U|A|U^{*}+V|B|V^{*}+W|C|W^{*},

for some isometries
U,V,W
, and

\mu_{4}^{2}(\mathbb{A})\leq\mu_{3}^{2}(A)+\mu_{2}^{2}(B)+\mu_{1}^{2}(C)

where
\mu_{j}
stands for the
j
-th singular value. Our theorem may be used to extend a result by Bhatia and Kittaneh for the
Schatten
p
-noms and to give a singular value version of Cauchy’s Interlacing Theorem.

Keywords.
Partitioned matrices, functional calculus, matrix inequalities.

2010 mathematics subject classification.
15A18, 15A60, 47A30.

8.1
Introduction

Let
\mathbb{M}_{d}
denote the space of
d
-by-
d
matrices. If
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
, the polar decomposition holds,

\mathbb{A}=U|\mathbb{A}|

(8.1.1)

where
|\mathbb{A}|\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
is positive semi-definite and
U\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
is a unitary matrix. The matrix
|\mathbb{A}|
is called the absolute
value of
\mathbb{A}
, and its
eigenvalues are the singular values of
\mathbb{A}
. The absolute value can be defined for
d\times d^{\prime}
matrices
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
as a
positive matrix
|\mathbb{A}|\in\mathbb{M}_{d^{\prime}}
, and the factor
U
in (
8.1.1
) is an isometry (
d\geq d^{\prime}
) or a coisometry (
d<d^{\prime}
).

If
\mathbb{A}
is partitioned in some number of rectangular blocks, say four blocks
A,B,C,D
, it is of interest to have a relation between
the absolute value
|\mathbb{A}|
and the absolute values of the blocks. By using the standard inner product of
\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
, we immediately
have the trace relation

{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|\mathbb{A}|^{2}={\mathrm{Tr\,}}|A|^{2}+{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|B|^{2}+{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|C|^{2}+{\mathrm{Tr\,}}|D|^{2}.

This note aims to point out a much stronger Pythagorean theorem, Theorem
8.2.1
, and several consequences. This result holds
for many partitionings of
\mathbb{A}
, not only when
A
is a block matrix partitioned into
p\times q
blocks. For instance, one may
consider the matrix

\mathbb{A}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}\\
a_{3}&a_{4}&b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
a_{5}&a_{6}&c_{1}&c_{2}&d_{1}\\
a_{7}&a_{8}&c_{3}&c_{4}&d_{2}\\
a_{9}&a_{10}&c_{5}&c_{6}&d_{3}\end{pmatrix}

partitioned into four obvious blocks
A,B,C,D
.

If
\mathbb{A}
is partitioned into
r
blocks
A_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
, we write

\mathbb{A}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{r}A_{k}=A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{r}

(8.1.2)

where we can use the
=
sign if one considers
A_{k}
not only as an element of
\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
but also as a submatrix of

\mathbb{A}
with its position in
\mathbb{A}
.

We say that the partitioning (
8.1.2
) is column compatible, or that
\mathbb{A}
is partitioned into column compatible blocks if for all
pairs of indexes
k,l
, either
A_{k}
and
A_{l}
lie on the same set of columns of
\mathbb{A}
, or
A_{k}
and
A_{l}
lie on two disjoint sets
of columns of
\mathbb{A}
. Similarly, (
8.1.2
) is row compatible, if for all pairs of indexes
k,l
, either
A_{k}
and
A_{l}
lie on the
same set of rows of
\mathbb{A}
, or
A_{k}
and
A_{l}
lie on two disjoint sets of rows of
\mathbb{A}
.

Our Pythagorean Theorem
8.2.1
will be stated for row or column compatible blocks. An application is a Theorem of Bhatia and
Kittaneh for the Schatten
p
-norms (Corollary
8.2.5
). Another application is an inequality for the singular values of
compression onto hyperplanes. A matrix
A\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
is an operator on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
. Given a hyperplane
{\mathcal{S}}
of
\mathbb{C}^{d}
, we have
a unit vector
h
such that
{\mathcal{S}}=h^{\perp}
, that is
x\in{\mathcal{S}}\iff\langle h,x\rangle=h^{*}x=0
. The compression
A_{\mathcal{S}}
of
A
onto

{\mathcal{S}}
is the operator acting on
{\mathcal{S}}
defined as the restriction of
EA
to
{\mathcal{S}}
where
E
stands for
the (orthogonal) projection onto
{\mathcal{S}}
. Theorem
8.2.1
entails a bound for the singular values of
A_{\mathcal{S}}

in terms of those of
A
. These results are given in Section 3; we state a special case in the following corollary. Let
\mu_{j}

denote the
j
-th singular value arranged in nonincreasing order.

Corollary 8.1.1
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
be a normal matrix and let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a hyperplane of
\mathbb{C}^{d}
orthogonal to the unit vector
h
. Set
\beta=\|Ah\|^{2}-|\langle h,Ah\rangle|^{2}
. Then,
for
j=1,\ldots,d-1
,

\mu_{j}^{2}(A)\geq\mu_{j}^{2}(A_{\mathcal{S}})\geq\mu^{2}_{j+1}(A)-\beta.

We discuss the case of four and five blocks in Section 4. For four blocks, our Pythagorean theorem entails an interesting inequality
stated in the next corollary.

Corollary 8.1.2
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
be partitioned into four blocks
A,B,C,D
. Then, there exist some isometries

U,V,W,X
of suitable sizes such that

2|\mathbb{A}|\geq U|A|U^{*}+V|B|V^{*}+W|C|W^{*}+X|D|X^{*}.

The last section is devoted to several other operator inequalities such as the first inequality in the abstract.

8.2
A Pythagorean theorem

Theorem 8.2.1
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
be partitioned into
r
row or column compatible blocks
A_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
.
Then, there exist some isometries
U_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{d^{\prime},m_{k}}
such that

|\mathbb{A}|^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}U_{k}|A_{k}|^{2}U_{k}^{*}.

Recall that
U\in\mathbb{M}_{d^{\prime},m}
,
m\leq d^{\prime}
, is an isometry if
U^{*}U=\mathbb{1}_{m}
, the identity on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. If

\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,1}
, then the theorem reads as Pythagoras’ Theorem.

Proof.

Consider a positive matrix in
\mathbb{M}_{n+m}
partitioned as

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}

with diagonal blocks
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and
B\in\mathbb{M}_{m}
. By
[
31
, Lemma 3.4]
we have two unitary matrices
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n+m}
such that

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}=U\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}U^{*}+V\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}V^{*},

(8.2.1)

equivalently,

\begin{bmatrix}A&X\\
X^{*}&B\end{bmatrix}=U_{1}AU_{1}^{*}+U_{2}BU_{2}^{*}

for two isometry matrices
U_{1}\in\mathbb{M}_{n+m,n}
and
U_{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{n+m,m}
. An obvious iteration of (
8.2.1
) shows that, given a
positive block matrix in
\mathbb{M}_{m}
partitioned into
p\times p
blocks,

\mathbb{B}=\left(B_{i,j}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq p},

with square diagonal blocks
B_{i,i}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{i}}
and
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{p}=m
, we have the decomposition

\mathbb{B}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}U_{i}B_{i,i}U_{i}^{*}

(8.2.2)

for some isometries
U_{i}\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n_{i}}
.

We use (
8.2.2
) to prove the theorem. Consider first the column compatible case. Thus we have a partitioning into
p
block
columns,

\mathbb{A}={\mathbf{C}}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathbf{C}}_{p},

(8.2.3)

and each block
A_{k}
belongs to one block column
{\mathbf{C}}_{q}
. By relabelling the
A_{k}
’s if necessary, we may assume that we
have
p
integers
1=\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\cdots<\alpha_{p}<r
such that

{\mathbf{C}}_{q}=A_{\alpha_{q}}\cup\cdots\cup A_{\alpha_{q+1}-1},\hskip 9.24994pt1\leq q<p,\hskip 9.24994pt{\text{and}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\mathbf{C}}_{p}=A_{\alpha_{p}}\cup\cdots\cup A_{\alpha_{r}}.

We also have a partitioning into
p
block rows,

\mathbb{A}^{*}={\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{p},

(8.2.4)

and multiplying (
8.2.4
) and (
8.2.3
) we then obtain a block matrix for
\mathbb{A}^{*}\mathbb{A}=|\mathbb{A}|^{2}\in\mathbb{M}_{d^{\prime}}
,

|\mathbb{A}|^{2}=\left({\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{i}{\mathbf{C}}_{j}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq p}.

By the decomposition (
8.2.2
) we have

|\mathbb{A}|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}U_{i}{\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{i}{\mathbf{C}}_{i}U_{i}^{*}

for some isometries
U_{i}\in\mathbb{M}_{d^{\prime},n_{i}}
, where
n_{i}
is the number of columns of
{\mathbf{C}}_{i}
. Hence, with the convention

\alpha_{p+1}:=r+1
,

|\mathbb{A}|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{k=\alpha_{i}}^{\alpha_{i+1}-1}U_{i}A_{k}^{*}A_{k}U_{i}^{*}

establishing the theorem for a column compatible partitioning.

Now, we turn to the row compatible case. Thus we have a partitioning into
p
block rows,

\mathbb{A}={\mathbf{R}}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathbf{R}}_{p},

(8.2.5)

and each block
A_{k}
belongs to one block row
{\mathbf{R}}_{q}
and, as in the column compatible case, we may assume that we have
p

integers
1=\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\cdots<\alpha_{p}<r
such that

{\mathbf{R}}_{q}=A_{\alpha_{q}}\cup\cdots\cup A_{\alpha_{q+1}-1},\hskip 9.24994pt1\leq q<p,\hskip 9.24994pt{\text{and}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\mathbf{R}}_{p}=A_{\alpha_{p}}\cup\cdots\cup A_{\alpha_{r}}.

We also have a partitioning into
p
block columns,

\mathbb{A}^{*}={\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{p}

(8.2.6)

Mutiply (
8.2.6
) and (
8.2.5
) and note that

|\mathbb{A}|^{2}=\sum_{l=1}^{p}{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{l}{\mathbf{R}}_{l}.

(8.2.7)

with
p
block matrices in
\mathbb{M}_{d^{\prime}}
, (
l=1,\ldots,p
),

{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{l}{\mathbf{R}}_{l}=\left(A^{*}_{i}A_{j}\right)_{\alpha_{l}\leq i,j<\alpha_{l+1}}

(8.2.8)

where we still use
\alpha_{p+1}:=r+1
. Applying the decomposition (
8.2.2
) to the block matrices
(
8.2.8
) yields

{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{l}{\mathbf{R}}_{l}=\sum_{i=\alpha_{l}}^{\alpha_{l+1}-1}U_{i}|A_{i}|^{2}U_{i}^{*}

for some isometries
U_{i}
of suitable sizes,
and combining with (
8.2.7
) completes the proof. ∎

Denote by
\mu_{1}(S)\geq\mu_{2}(S)\geq\cdots
the singular values of a matrix
S\in\mathbb{M}_{n,m}
. This list is often limited to

\min\{n,m\}
elements, however we can naturally define
\mu_{k}(S)=0
for any index
k
larger than
\min\{n,m\}
. Given two matrices
of same size, a classical inequality of Weyl asserts that

\mu_{j+k+1}(S+T)\leq\mu_{j+1}(S)+\mu_{k+1}(T)

for all nonnegative integers
j
and
k
. This inequality and Theorem
8.2.1
entail the next corollary.

Corollary 8.2.2
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
be partitioned into
r
row or column compatible blocks
A_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
.
Then, for all nonnegative integers
j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{r}
,

\mu^{2}_{j_{1}+j_{2}+\cdots+j_{r}+1}(\mathbb{A})\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r}\mu^{2}_{j_{k}+1}(A_{k}).

A special case of this inequality is given in the abstract for three blocks and
j_{1}=2
,
j_{2}=1
,
j_{3}=0
.

Since any partitioning into three blocks is row or column compatible we have the next corollary.

Corollary 8.2.3
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
be partitioned into three blocks
A,B,C
. Then, there exist some isometries

U,V,W
of suitable sizes such that

|\mathbb{A}|^{2}=U|A|^{2}U^{*}+V|B|^{2}V^{*}+W|C|^{2}W^{*}.

By using the triangle inequality for the Schatten
p
-norms we have the trace inequality

\left\{{\mathrm{Tr}}\,|\mathbb{A}|^{2p}\right\}^{1/p}\leq\left\{{\mathrm{Tr}}\,|A|^{2p}\right\}^{1/p}+\left\{{\mathrm{Tr}}\,|B|^{2p}\right\}^{1/p}+\left\{{\mathrm{Tr}}\,|C|^{2p}\right\}^{1/p},\hskip 9.24994ptp\geq 1,

(8.2.9)

equivalently

\|\mathbb{A}\|^{2}_{q}\leq\|A\|^{2}_{q}+\|B\|^{2}_{q}+\|C\|^{2}_{q}

(8.2.10)

for all Schatten
q
-norms,
q\geq 2
.

Theorem
8.2.1
entails another interesting relation between the blocks of a partitioned matrix and the full matrix.

Corollary 8.2.4
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
be partitioned into
r
row or column compatible blocks
A_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
.
Then, for some isometries
V_{j}\in\mathbb{M}_{m,d^{\prime}}
, with
m=\sum_{k=1}^{r}m_{k}
,

\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}|A_{k}|^{2}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{r}V_{j}|\mathbb{A}|^{2}V_{j}^{*}.

Proof.

From Theorem
8.2.1
and the main result of
[
35
]
we have

\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}U_{k}|A_{k}|^{2}U_{k}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{r}W_{j}|\mathbb{A}|^{2}W_{j}^{*}

for some isometries
U_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{d^{\prime},m_{k}}
and some isometries
W_{j}\in\mathbb{M}_{rd^{\prime},d^{\prime}}
. Since

\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}|A_{k}|^{2}=C\left\{\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}U_{k}|A_{k}|^{2}U_{k}\right\}C^{*}

for some contraction
C\in\mathbb{M}_{m,rd^{\prime}}
, we infer

\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}|A_{k}|^{2}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{r}CW_{j}|\mathbb{A}|^{2}W_{j}^{*}C^{*}.

If
|\mathbb{A}|
is invertible, then, taking trace, the above equality ensures that contractions
CW_{j}
satisfy

W_{j}^{*}C^{*}CW_{j}={\mathbf{1}}_{d^{\prime}}
for all
j
. Hence the result is proved with
V_{j}=CW_{j}
. The general case follows by a limit
argument.
∎

We are in a position to estimate the Schatten norms of the blocks with the full matrix.
The following corollary was first obtained by Bhatia and Kittaneh
[
17
]
in case of a matrix partitioned into
n\times n

blocks.

Corollary 8.2.5
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
be partitioned into
r
row or column compatible blocks
A_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
.
Then, for all Schatten
q
-norms,
q\geq 2
,

r^{{\frac{2}{q}}-1}\sum_{k=1}^{r}\|A_{k}\|_{q}^{2}\leq\|\mathbb{A}\|_{q}^{2}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r}\|A_{k}\|_{q}^{2}

These two inequalities are reversed for
2>q>0
.

Proof.

For
p:=q/2\geq 1
, the second inequality contains (
8.2.10
) and immediately follows from Theorem
8.2.1
and
the triangle inequality for the Schatten
p
-norms. Corollary
8.2.4
gives

\||\mathbb{A}|^{2}\|_{p}\geq\left\||A_{1}|^{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus|A_{r}|^{2}\right\|_{p}

and since the concavity of
t\mapsto t^{1/p}
entails

\left\||A_{1}|^{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus|A_{r}|^{2}\right\|_{p}=\left(\||A_{1}|^{2}\|_{p}^{p}+\cdots\||A_{r}|^{2}\|_{p}^{p}\right)^{1/p}\geq r^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\left(\||A_{1}|^{2}\|_{p}+\cdots+\||A_{r}|^{2}\|_{p}\right)

we get the first inequality. These inequalities are reversed for
0<p<1
.
∎

Corollary
8.2.4
is relevant to Majorisation Theory. We take this opportunity to point out an interesting
fact about majorisation in the next proposition. Though this result might be well-known to some experts, it does not seem to be in
the literature. Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
, the positive semi-definite cone of
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. The majorisation
A\prec B
means that

\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mu_{j}(A)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mu_{j}(B)

for all
k=1,2,\ldots n
, with equality for
k=n
.
The majorisation
A\prec B
is equivalent to

A=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}U_{i}BU_{i}^{*}

for some unitary matrices
U_{i}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
and weights
\alpha_{i}\geq 0
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}=1
. This can be easily derived from
Caratheodory’s theorem
[
94
]
. A more accurate statement holds.

Proposition 8.2.6
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
,
A\prec B
.Then, for some unitary matrices
U_{i}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

A=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}BU_{i}^{*}.

Proof.

By the Schur-Horn Theorem, we may assume that
A
is the diagonal part of
B
. Then we use the simple idea of Equation
(2) in the nice paper of Bhatia
[
14
]
.
∎

Note that Corollary
8.2.4
may be restated as

\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}|A_{k}|^{2}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{r}U_{j}(|\mathbb{A}|^{2}\oplus O)U_{j}^{*}.

for some unitary matrices
U_{j}
and some fixed zero matrix
O
. Hence we have an average of
r
matrices in the unitary orbit of

|\mathbb{A}|^{2}\oplus O
, this number
r
being (much) smaller than the one given by Proposition
8.2.6
,
d=m_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}
.

8.3
Compression onto a hyperplane

By a hyperplane of
\mathbb{C}^{d}
we mean a vector subspace of dimension
d-1
. The next corollary is a singular value version of Cauchy’s
Interlacing Theorem
[
13
, p. 59]
.

Corollary 8.3.1
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
and let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a hyperplane of
\mathbb{C}^{d}
orthogonal to the unit vector
h
.
Set
\beta=\min\{\|Ah\|^{2},\|A^{*}h\|^{2}\}-|\langle h,Ah\rangle|^{2}
. Then, for all
j=1,\ldots,d-1
,

\mu_{j}^{2}(A)\geq\mu_{j}^{2}(A_{\mathcal{S}})\geq\mu_{j+1}^{2}(A)-\beta.

This double inequality is stronger than
\mu_{j}(A)\geq\mu_{j}(A_{\mathcal{S}})\geq\mu_{j+1}(A)-\sqrt{\beta}
. If
A
is a normal
matrix, then
\|Ah\|=\|A^{*}h\|
and we have Corollary
8.1.1
. If
A=V
is a unitary matrix,
\mu_{j}(V)=1
for all
j
and
\|Vh\|=\|V^{*}h\|=1
for all unit vectors, so we deduce from Corollary
8.3.1
that

\mu_{j}(V_{\mathcal{S}})\geq|\langle h,Vh\rangle|
. In fact one can easily check that
\mu_{j}(V_{\mathcal{S}})=1
for
j\leq d-2

and
\mu_{d-1}(V_{\mathcal{S}})=|\langle h,Vh\rangle|
. Hence Corollary
8.3.1
is sharp.

Proof.

(Corollary
8.3.1
) The inequality
\mu_{j}(A)\geq\mu_{j}(A_{\mathcal{S}})
is trivial. To deal with the other
inequality
we may assume that
h
is the last vector of the canonical basis and that
A_{\mathcal{S}}
is the submatrix of
A
obtained by
deleting the last column and the last line. We partition
A
as

A=A_{\mathcal{S}}\cup B\cup C

where
B
contains the
d-1
entries below
A_{\mathcal{S}}
and
C
is the last column of
A
. We then apply to this partitioning
Corollary
8.2.2
with
j_{1}=j-1
,
j_{2}=0
, and
j_{3}=1
to get

\mu_{j+1}^{2}(A)\leq\mu_{j}^{2}(A_{\mathcal{S}})+\mu_{1}^{2}(B)+\mu_{2}^{2}(C).

Since
\mu_{2}(C)=0
, we have

\ \mu_{j+1}^{2}(A)-\mu_{1}^{2}(B)\leq\mu_{j}^{2}(A_{\mathcal{S}})

Observe that
\mu_{1}^{2}(B)=\|A^{*}h\|^{2}-|\langle h,Ah\rangle|^{2}
, hence

\mu_{j}^{2}(A_{\mathcal{S}})\geq\mu_{j+1}^{2}(A)-\|A^{*}h\|^{2}+|\langle h,Ah\rangle|^{2}.

(8.3.1)

We may also partition
A
as

A=A_{\mathcal{S}}\cup R\cup L

where
R
contains the
d-1
entries on the right of
A_{\mathcal{S}}
and
L
stands for the last line of
A
.
Arguing as above with
R
and
L
in place of
B
and
C
yields

\mu_{j+1}^{2}(A)-\mu_{j}^{2}(A_{\mathcal{S}})\leq\mu_{1}^{2}(R)=\|Ah\|^{2}-|\langle h,Ah\rangle|^{2}

(8.3.2)

Combining (
8.3.1
) and (
8.3.2
) completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 8.3.2
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
be partitioned into
r
row or column compatible blocks
A_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
.
Then, for each block
A_{k}
and all
j\geq 1
,

\mu^{2}_{j}(\mathbb{A})-\mu^{2}_{j}(A_{k})\leq\sum_{l\neq k}\mu^{2}_{1}(A_{l}).

Proof.

Apply Corollary
8.2.2
with
j_{k}=j-1
and
j_{l}=0
for all
l\neq k
. ∎

Corollary 8.3.3
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
and let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a hyperplane of
\mathbb{C}^{d}
orthogonal to the unit vector
h
.
Then for all
j=1,\ldots,d-1
,

\mu^{2}_{j}(A)-\mu^{2}_{j}(A_{\mathcal{S}})\leq\|Ah\|^{2}+\|A^{*}h\|^{2}-|\langle h,Ah\rangle|^{2}.

Proof.

We may suppose that
h
is the last vector of the canonical basis and we partition
A
into three blocks :
A_{\mathcal{S}}
, the
last column of
A
, and the
d-1
entries below
A_{\mathcal{S}}
. We then apply the previous corollary.
∎

8.4
Four and five blocks

Partitionings into four blocks are not necessarily row or column compatible. However, for such partitionings, Theorem
8.2.1

still holds.

Corollary 8.4.1
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
be partitioned into four blocks
A,B,C,D
. Then, there exist some isometries

U,V,W,X
of suitable sizes such that

|\mathbb{A}|^{2}=U|A|^{2}U^{*}+V|B|^{2}V^{*}+W|C|^{2}W^{*}+X|D|^{2}X^{*}.

Proof.

We assume that
A
is the block in the upper left corner and we distinguish three cases.

(1)

A
has the same number
d
of lines as
\mathbb{A}
.
In such a case, letting
A^{\prime}=B\cup C\cup D
, the partitioning
\mathbb{A}=A\cup A^{\prime}
is column compatible, and we have two isometry matrices

U,U^{\prime}
such that

|\mathbb{A}|^{2}=U|A|^{2}U^{*}+U^{\prime}|A^{\prime}|^{2}U^{\prime*}.

(8.4.1)

Since
A^{\prime}
is partitioned into three blocks, necessarily a row or column partitioning, we can apply the theorem to obtain the
decomposition

|A^{\prime}|^{2}=V^{\prime}|B|^{2}V^{\prime*}+W^{\prime}|B|^{2}W^{\prime*}+X^{\prime}|B|^{2}X^{\prime*}

(8.4.2)

for some isometry matrices
V^{\prime},W^{\prime},X^{\prime}
of suitable sizes. Combining (
8.4.1
) and (
8.4.2
) we get the conclusion of the corollary
with the isometry matrices
V=U^{\prime}V^{\prime}
,
W=U^{\prime}W^{\prime}
, and
X=U^{\prime}X^{\prime}
.

(2)

A
has the same number
d^{\prime}
of columns as
\mathbb{A}
.
Letting again
A^{\prime}=B\cup C\cup D
, the partitioning
\mathbb{A}=A\cup A^{\prime}
is row compatible, and we may argue as in case (1).

(3)

A
has
l<d
lines and
c<d^{\prime}
columns. There exist then a block, say
B
, on the top position, and just on the right of
A
, and
another block, say
C
just below
A
and on the left side. We consider three subcases (a), (b), (c).

(a)
B
has fewer than
l
lines. Then, the last block
D
is necessarily below
B
with the same number of columns as
B
, and so

C
has either the same number of columns as
A
or
C
has
d^{\prime}
columns as
\mathbb{A}
. In the first case,
\mathbb{A}=A\cup C\cup B\cup D
is
a column compatible partitioning and we can apply the theorem. In the second case, the situation is the same as in (2).

(b)
B
has exactly
l
lines, like
A
. We denote by
\gamma
the number of columns of
B
and we consider three situations.

(i)
C
has more than
c+\gamma
columns. Then necessarily
C
has
d^{\prime}
columns and
D
is the upper right block with
l
lines,
hence
\mathbb{A}=A\cup B\cup D\cup C
is a line compatible partitioning and we may apply the theorem.

(ii)
C
has exactly
c+\gamma
columns. Then, letting
\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}=A\cup B\cup C
with have a partitioning into three blocks, and
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}\cup D
. Thus applying the theorem twice as in case (1) yields the conclusion.

(iii)
C
has fewer than
c+\gamma
columns. Then
D
is the lower right block, with the same number of lines as
B
, and
\mathbb{A}
is
partitioned into line compatible blocks. Thus the theorem can be applied.

(c)
B
has more lines than
A
. Let
\lambda
be the number of line of
B
. Hence
\lambda>l
. There exist two situations

(iv)
\lambda<d
. Then
D
is the lower right block, with the same number of columns as
B
, and
\mathbb{A}
is partitioned into line
compatible blocks. Thus we may apply the theorem.

(v)
\lambda=d
. Then
A^{\prime\prime\prime}=A\cup C\cup D
is a partitioning into three blocks and
\mathbb{A}=A^{\prime\prime\prime}\cup B
, thus applying twice the
theorem completes the proof.
∎

We do not know whether Corollary
8.4.1
can be extended or not to any partitioning in five blocks. For instance we are not
able to prove or disprove a version of Corollary
8.4.1
for the matrices

\mathbb{A}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&b_{1}\\
d_{1}&x&b_{2}\\
d_{2}&c_{1}&c_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\hskip 9.24994pt{\mathrm{or}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbb{A}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}&b_{1}&b_{2}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}&b_{3}&b_{4}\\
d_{1}&d_{2}&x&b_{5}&b_{6}\\
d_{3}&d_{4}&c_{1}&c_{2}&c_{3}\\
d_{5}&d_{6}&c_{4}&c_{5}&c_{6}\end{pmatrix}

partitioned into five obvious blocks
A,B,C,D,X
. Hence, that Theorem
8.2.1
holds or not for any partitioning into five
blocks is an open problem. More generally, we may consider the following two questions.

Question 8.4.2
.

For which partitionings does Theorem
8.2.1
hold ? For which partitionings does Corollary
8.2.2
hold
?

Matrices partitioned into four blocks (usually of same size) are comon examples of partitionings. A nontrivial inequality follows
from the previous corollary.

Corollary 8.4.3
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
be partitioned into four blocks
A,B,C,D
, and let
p>2
. Then, there exist
some isometries
U,V,W,X
of suitable sizes such that

2^{2-p}|\mathbb{A}|^{p}\leq U|A|^{p}U^{*}+V|B|^{p}V^{*}+W|C|^{p}W^{*}+X|D|^{p}X^{*}.

The inequality reverses for
2>p>0
.

Letting
p=1
we have Corollary
8.1.2
with the constant 2 which is sharp, even for a positive block matrix, as shown by
the simple example

\mathbb{A}=\begin{bmatrix}A&A\\
A&A\end{bmatrix}.

Proof.

For any monotone convex function
f(t)
on the nonnegative axis, we have thanks to
[
31
, Corollary 2.4]
and Corollary

8.4.1
,

\displaystyle f\left(\frac{|\mathbb{A}|^{2}}{4}\right)

\displaystyle=f\left(\frac{U|A|^{2}U^{*}+V|B|^{2}V^{*}+W|C|^{2}W^{*}+X|D|^{2}X^{*}}{4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\frac{f(U|A|^{2}U^{*})+V(f|B|^{2}V^{*})+f(W|C|^{2}W^{*})+f(X|D|^{2}X^{*})}{4}\Lambda^{*}

for some unitary matrix
\Lambda\in\mathbb{M}_{d}^{\prime}
. Picking
f(t)=t^{p/2}
with
p>2
yields the result. The reverse inequalities hold for
monotone concave functions
f(t)
and
0<p<2
.
∎

Remark 8.4.4
.

The version of Corollary
8.4.3
for three blocks
A,B,C
, and
p=1
reads as the inequality of the abstract,

\sqrt{3}|\mathbb{A}|\geq U|A|U^{*}+V|B|V^{*}+W|C|W^{*}.

The constant
\sqrt{3}
is the best one: we cannot take a smaller constant for

\mathbb{A}=\begin{bmatrix}x&y&z\\
x&y&z\\
x&y&z\end{bmatrix}

partitioned into its three lines. For two blocks, a similar sharp inequality holds with the constant
\sqrt{2}
.

8.5
Concave or convex functions

For sake of simplicity we state our results for a square matrix
\mathbb{A}
partitioned into blocks. By adding some zero rows or zero
columns to a rectangular matrix, we could obtain statements for rectangular matrices (Remark
8.5.9
).

Suppose that
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
is partitioned into blocks
A_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
,
k=1,\ldots,r
. From Thompson’s triangle inequality
(
[
92
]
or
[
13
, p. 74]
we have

|\mathbb{A}|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r}U_{k}|A_{k}|U_{k}^{*}

(8.5.1)

for some isometry matrices
U_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,m_{k}}
. The equality of Theorem
8.2.1
and (
8.5.1
) suggest several other
inequalities, in particular, if
\mathbb{A}
is partioned in row or column compatible blocks,

|\mathbb{A}|^{3}\geq\sum_{k=1}^{r}V_{k}|A_{k}|^{3}V_{k}^{*}

(8.5.2)

for some isometries
V_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,m_{k}}
. This is indeed true as shown in the following theorem. We do not know if (
8.5.2
) can be
extended to any partitioning. Corollary
8.4.1
and the proof of Theorem
8.5.1
show that (
8.5.2
) holds for four
blocks. The case of five blocks is open.

Theorem 8.5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
be partitioned into
r
row or column compatible blocks
A_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
,
and let
\psi(t)
be a monotone function on
[0,\infty)
such that
\psi(\sqrt{t})
is convex and
\psi(0)=0
. Then there exist some
isometries
V_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,m_{k}}
such that

\psi(|\mathbb{A}|)\geq\sum_{k=1}^{r}V_{k}\psi(|A_{k}|)V_{k}^{*}.

Theorem
8.5.1
considerably improves (
8.2.9
). A special case with
\psi(t)=t^{3}
is given in the abstract.

Proof.

Let
g(t)
be a monotone convex function on
[0,\infty)
such that
g(0)\leq 0
, and let
A,B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
be positive
(semidefinite). By
[
12
]
or
[
31
, Corollary 3.2]
we have

g(A+B)\geq Ug(A)U^{*}+Vg(B)V^{*}

for some unitary matrices
U,V\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Using this inequality and Theorem
8.2.1
we infer

g(|\mathbb{A}|^{2})\geq\sum_{k=1}^{r}W_{k}g(U_{k}|A_{k}|^{2}U_{k}^{*})W_{k}^{*}

for some unitary matrices
W_{k}
and some isometry matrices
U_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,m_{k}}
. If
g(0)=0
, we have

g(U_{k}|A_{k}|^{2}U_{k}^{*})=U_{k}g(|A_{k}|^{2})U_{k}^{*}
. Hence

g(|\mathbb{A}|^{2})\geq\sum_{k=1}^{r}V_{k}g(|A_{k}|^{2})V_{k}^{*}

with the isometry matrices
V_{k}=W_{k}U_{k}
. Applying this to
g(t)=\psi(\sqrt{t})
completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 8.5.2
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d}
be partitioned into
r
row or column compatible blocks
A_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{k},m_{k}}
,
and let
\varphi(t)
be a nonnegative function on
[0,\infty)
such that
\varphi(\sqrt{t})
is concave. Then there exist some
isometries
U_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,n_{k}}
such that

\varphi(|\mathbb{A}|)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r}U_{k}\varphi(|A_{k}|)U_{k}^{*}.

Proof.

Since
\varphi(\sqrt{t})
is nonnegative and concave, it is necessarily a monotone function (nondecreasing), hence
continuous on
(0,\infty)
. Since we are dealing with matrices we may further suppose that
\varphi(t)
is also continuous at
t=0
.

(1) Assume that
\varphi(0)=0
. Theorem
8.5.1
applied to
\psi(t)=-\varphi(t)
proves the corollary.

(2) Assume that
\varphi(0)>0
. Since the continuous functional calculus is continuous on the positive semidefinite cone of any

\mathbb{M}_{m}
, by a limit argument, we may assume that
|\mathbb{A}|
is invertible. So, suppose that the spectrum of
|\mathbb{A}|^{2}
lies in an
interval
[r^{2},s^{2}]
with
r>0
.
Define a convex function
\phi(\sqrt{t})
by
\phi(\sqrt{t})=\varphi(\sqrt{t})
for
t\geq r^{2}
,
\phi(0)=0
, and the graph of

\phi(\sqrt{t})
on
[0,r^{2}]
is a line segment. Hence
\phi(t)\leq\varphi(t)
and
\phi(|\mathbb{A}|)=\varphi(|\mathbb{A}|)
. Applying case (1) to

\phi
yields

\varphi(|\mathbb{A}|)=\phi(|\mathbb{A}|)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r}U_{k}\phi(|A_{k}|)U_{k}^{*}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r}U_{k}\varphi(|A_{k}|)U_{k}^{*}

for some isometry matrices
U_{k}
.
∎

The next three corollaries follow from Corollary
8.5.2
.

Corollary 8.5.3
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{mn}
be partitioned into an
m\times m
family of blocks
A_{i,j}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, and let
0<q\leq 2
. Then there exist some isometries
U_{i,j}\in\mathbb{M}_{mn,n}
such that

|\mathbb{A}|^{q}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}U_{i,j}|A_{i,j}|^{q}U_{i,j}^{*}.

Corollary 8.5.4
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{mn}
be partitioned into an
m\times m
family of blocks
A_{i,j}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, let
s\geq 1

and
0<q\leq 2
. Then,

\left\{{\mathrm{Tr}\,}|\mathbb{A}|^{qs}\right\}^{1/s}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left\{{\mathrm{Tr}\,}|A_{i,j}|^{qs}\right\}^{1/s}.

Corollary 8.5.5
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, let
c_{k}
be the norm of the
k
-th column of
A
and let
0<q\leq 2
. Then there exist
some rank one projections
E_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
such that

|\mathbb{A}|^{q}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}^{q}E_{k}

The last corollaries follow from Theorem
8.5.1
.

Corollary 8.5.6
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{mn}
be partitioned into an
m\times m
family of blocks
A_{i,j}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, and let
p\geq 2
. Then there exist some isometries
U_{i,j}\in\mathbb{M}_{mn,n}
such that

|\mathbb{A}|^{p}\geq\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}U_{i,j}|A_{i,j}|^{p}U_{i,j}^{*}.

Corollary 8.5.7
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{mn}
be partitioned into an
m\times m
family of blocks
A_{i,j}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, let
0\leq s\leq 1
and
p\geq 2
. Then,

\left\{{\mathrm{Tr}\,}|\mathbb{A}|^{ps}\right\}^{1/s}\geq\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left\{{\mathrm{Tr}\,}|A_{i,j}|^{ps}\right\}^{1/s}.

Corollary 8.5.8
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
, let
r_{k}
be the norm of the
k
-th row of
A
and let
p\geq 2
. Then there exist some
rank one projections
E_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
such that

|\mathbb{A}|^{p}\geq\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}^{p}E_{k}.

Remark 8.5.9
.

The proof of Theorem
8.5.1
is the same for a
d\times d^{\prime}
matrix
\mathbb{A}
. So Corollary

8.5.2
also holds for
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{d,d^{\prime}}
if
\varphi(0)=0
. In case of
d\geq d^{\prime}
, we may again use a limit argument and
assume that
|\mathbb{A}|
is invertible. In case of
d^{\prime}>d
we may argue as follows. Add some zero lines to
\mathbb{A}
in order to obtain
a square matrix
\mathbb{A}_{0}\in\mathbb{M}_{d}^{\prime}
. Let
B_{1}\ldots,B_{p}
be the blocks at the bottom of
\mathbb{A}
, and
R_{1},\ldots,R_{q}
be the remaining
blocks of
\mathbb{A}
. Add some zeros to the blocks
B_{i}
in order to obtain blocks
B_{i}^{0}
of
\mathbb{A}_{0}
in such a way that

\mathbb{A}_{0}=\left(\bigcup_{i}B_{i}^{0}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{j}R_{j}\right)

is a row or column compatible partitioning of
\mathbb{A}_{0}
. Since it is a square matrix, we may apply Corollary
8.5.2
and
since

|\mathbb{A}_{0}|=|\mathbb{A}|
and
|B_{i}^{0}|=|B^{i}|
, we see that Corollary
8.5.2
holds for
d\times d^{\prime}
matrices.

8.6
Around this article

For a positive block-diagonal matrix, Theorem
8.2.1
is trivial. The following statement
[
35
]
is more interesting.

Theorem 8.6.1
.

Let
A_{i}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}^{+}
,
i\in\mathbb{I}_{m}
. Then, for some isometries
V_{k}\in\mathbb{M}_{mn,n}
,
k\in\mathbb{I}_{m}
,

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}A_{i}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}V_{k}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}A_{i}\right\}V_{k}^{*}.

This says that direct sums are averages of usual sums, up to isometric congruences. It is a genuine non-commutative fact with no analogous statement for positive vectors and permutations of their components.

Chapter 9
The spectral Theorem

9.1
Introduction

Hermitian (or symmetric) matrices acting on
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n}
(or
\mathbb{R}^{n}
) form a nice finite dimensional real vector space,
let us denote it by
\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
, with a order structure,
A\leq B
, whenever
B-A
is positive semidefinte.

The fundamental property of
\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}}_{n}
) is the
matrix spectral theorem
asserting that a Hermitian matrix can be
diagonalized in a suitable orthonormal basis. It is a very important theorem and, important too, it is an easy result well understood
by students. Combined with the order structure, this theorem shows that Hermitian matrices can be regarded as a generalization of

\mathbb{R}^{n}
and opens the way of a full theory of Matrix Analysis, where facts for real finite sequences of numbers search for
counterparts in the Hermitian matrix world.

But, the extension of this diagonalization theorem for operators on a infinite dimensional separable Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
-
a key stone result of Functional Analysis - is more delicate: the standard literature invoke abstract constructions based on the
Gelfand isomorphism between some abstract algebras. It might be a too abstract and unnatural approach; the link with matrices
unexpectedly disappears though operators obviously have matrix representations.

However, and fortunately as it is conceptually desirable, it is possible, to give a simple proof deriving from the matrix case. Hence
operators are not more complicated than matrices any longer ! We do this rather easy and definitely pleasant job in this note.

9.2
The matrix case, and two natural definitions for operators

Let us first recall a sketch of the proof
of the spectral theorem for Hermitian matrices.

•

if a subspace
{\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
is invariant by
A
, then so is its orthocomplement

{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}
.

•

\lambda_{1}^{\uparrow}(A):=\min\langle h,Ah\rangle
must be an eigenvalue,

so that repeating the process with the restriction of
A
to the orthocomplement …. This is the variational method. and we arrive
to the following spectral decomposition of
A
:

A=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{\uparrow}(A)P_{j}

(9.2.1)

A very important feature of this decompostion is to allow a matrix functional calculus, given
f(t)\in C^{0}
, the matrix
f(A)
makes
sense. This is the starting point of a branch of Matrix analysis. Note that the matrix functional calculus behave well with the order
structure of
C^{0}
,:
f(t)\geq g(t))
implies
f(A)\geq g(A)
for all Hermitian matrices
A
. this will be crucial in our approach.

Now, let us consider the typical situation in the infinite dimensional case, the multiplication operator on
L_{2}([0,1],

Z:f(t)\mapsto tf(t),\hskip 18.49988ptf(t)\in L_{2}([0,1],{\mathrm{d}}t)

This basic example shows that it is problematic to use eigenvalues and projection
P_{j}
as in (
9.2.1
). However it seems
possible to have a corresponding notion for the sum

E_{j}=P_{1}+P_{2}+\cdots P_{j},\hskip 18.49988ptj=1,2\cdots

(9.2.2)

of the first
j
spectral projections of
A
by using the operators

E_{w}:f(t)\mapsto{\mathbb{I}}_{[0,w]}(t)f(t),\hskip 18.49988ptw\in[0,1].

This is a very good point, because we may write the matrix spectral theorem (
9.2.1
) as:

A=\lambda_{1}^{\uparrow}(A)E_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{\uparrow}(A)(E_{2}-E_{1})+\lambda_{3}^{\uparrow}(A)(E_{3}-E_{2})+....+\lambda_{n}^{\uparrow}(A)(E_{n}-E_{n-1})

(9.2.3)

We will obtain a similar statement for Hilbert space operators.
To adapt the matrix proof to the infinite dimensional setting, we need two simple definitions. Let
\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
be the set of
Hermitian operators on a infinite dimensional, separable Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Definition 9.2.1
.

(sot-convergence, or strong convergence) We say that the sequence
\{X_{n}\}
in
\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
is sot
convergent, or strongly converges, if it is norm bounded and, for all vectors
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
(equivalently for all elements of
an orthonormal basis),
\{X_{n}h\}
converges in
{\mathcal{H}})
. If
\{X_{n}\}
is sot convergent, then there exists (a unic)
X\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
such that
Xh=\lim_{n}X_{n}h
for all vectors
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
and we say that
X
is the strong limit of

{X_{n}}
, written as
X={\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{n\to\infty}X_{n}
.

Hence the strong convergence is merely the pointwise convergence in
\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
. Of course, it is a classical notion and
”sot” refers to strong operator topology.

Given
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
, and fixing a (orthonormal) basis, we may write
A
as an infinite matrix
A=(a_{i,j})
, and so
extracting the
n
-by-
n
left upper corners,
n=1,2,3,....
we obtain a basic sequence of Hermitian matrices for which the matrix
spectral theorem and the functional calculus are available. Since we wish to extend it to
A
, it seems natural to use this sequence
of matrices, and the following terminology is convenient.

Definition 9.2.2
.

The sequence
\{A_{n}\}
in
\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
is a
basic sequence
for
A
if there exists an increasing
sequence of finite rank projections
\{E_{n}\}
, sot-convergent to the identity
I
, such that
A_{n}=E_{n}AE_{n}
.

9.3
Continuous functional calculus

In the introduction we have noted that the matrix spectral theorem allows to define the functional calculus of Hermitian matrices.
Our strategy in the Hilbert space operator case is opposite: we first built up a continuous functional calculus (thanks to the the
two above definition, and -of course- to the matrix spectral theorem)

Lemma 9.3.1
.

If
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a basic sequence for
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
and
p(t)
is a polynomial, then

{\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{n\to\infty}p(A_{n})=p(A).

Proof.

∎

Lemma 9.3.2
.

Let
f(t)\in C^{0}
and let
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
. Then, there exists an operator in

\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
, that we denote
f(A)
, such that

f(A)={\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{n\to\infty}f(A_{n})

(9.3.1)

for any basic sequence
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for
A
. Moreover
f(A)A=Af(A)
.

Thus, we have natural way to define operators
f(A)
from continuous function
f(t)
and Hermitian arguments
A
. This is called the
(operator) continous functional calculus. Lemma 2.1 shows that for a polynomial, the continous functional calculus yields, of course,
the same operator as its algebraic definition.

Proof.

Fix a unit vector
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
and a scalar
\varepsilon
, and pick a polynomial
p(t)
such that

|f(t)-p(t)|<\varepsilon

whenever
|t|\leq\|A\|
. Since
\|A_{n}\|\leq\|A\|
, for any
n
, we infer

\|f(A_{n})-p(A_{n})\|\leq\varepsilon.

(9.3.2)

Let
n_{0}
be an integer such that

\|p(A_{n})h-p(A)h\|\leq\varepsilon

(9.3.3)

for all
n\geq n_{0}
. Combining (
9.3.2
) and (
9.3.3
) we get

\|f(A_{n})h-p(A)h\|\leq 2\varepsilon

for all
n\geq n_{0}
. As
\varepsilon
can be arbitrarily small, we obtain that
\{f(A_{n})h\}
is a Cauchy sequence in
{\mathcal{H}}

and thus has a limit that we call
f(A)h
. It obviously defines a linear operator as
f(A_{n})
are linear operators. And since
\|f(A_{n})\|\leq\max\{|f(t)|:-\|A\|\leq t\leq\|A\|\}
,

f(A)
is a bounded linear operator. Thus we have the strong limit in
\mathbb{S}
,

f(A)={\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{n}f(A_{n}).

To check that
f(A)
does not depend on the basic sequence of
A
, suppose that
\{A^{\prime}_{n}\}
is another basic sequence of
A
and note
that we also have

\|f(A^{\prime}_{n})h-p(A)h\|\leq 2\varepsilon

for
n
large enough and thus
\{f^{\prime}(A_{n})\}
also converges to
f(A)h
. Finally, as
f(A_{n})A_{n}=A_{n}f(A_{n})
, taking the strong limits,
we infer
f(A)A=Af(A)
.
∎

For a finite rank operator
Z\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
,
{\mathrm{rank}}\,Z=m
, with spectral decomposition

Z=\sum_{j=1}^{m}z_{j}^{\uparrow}P_{j},

where
z_{j}
are the nonzero eigenvalues of
Z
counted with their multiplicities and
P_{j}
are corresponding rank one spectral
projections,
the functional calculus with
f\in C^{0}
is obtained by the simple formulae

f(Z)=f(0)P_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(z_{j}^{\uparrow})P_{j},

(9.3.4)

P_{0}
standing for the projection onto the nullspace of
Z
.
Therefore, given two continuous functions such that
f(t)\geq g(t)
, we have
f(Z)\geq g(Z)
. Applying this to a basic sequence we
obtain the following remark.

Remark 9.3.3
.

If
f(t),g(t)\in C^{0}
satysfy
f(t)\geq g(t)
, then
f(A)\geq g(A)
for all
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
.

The functional calculus for a finite rank operator (
9.3.4
) also shows that

\langle h,f(Z)f\rangle\leq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}f(t)

for all unit vectors
h
. Applying this to a basic sequence provides the next simple observation.

Remark 9.3.4
.

If
f(t)\in C^{0}
and
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
, then, for all unit vectors
h
,

\langle h,f(A)h\rangle\leq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}f(t).

9.4
Spectral projections

The aim of this section is to define the operator version of the matrix spectral projections (
9.2.2
). We first give a useful
fact, known as Vigier’s theorem.

Lemma 9.4.1
.

Let
\{Z_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a decreasing sequence of positive operators on
{\mathcal{H}}
, that is
Z_{n}\geq Z_{n+1}
for
all
n
. Then there exists a positive operator
Z
such that

Z={\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{n\to\infty}Z_{n}

Proof.

The wot convergence follows from polarization. Then, we may infer the sot convergence. Indeed, given

h\in{\mathcal{H}}
, one has

\displaystyle\|Z_{n+p}h-Z_{n}h\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle h,(Z_{n}-Z_{n+p})^{2}h\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle h,(Z_{n}-Z_{n+p})^{1/2}(Z_{n}-Z_{n+p})(Z_{n}-Z_{n+p})^{1/2}h\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|Z_{n}-Z_{n+p}\|\langle h,(Z_{n}-Z_{n+p})h\rangle.

∎

Lemma 9.4.2
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
. For
\lambda\in(-\infty,\infty)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, define the three piece affine
continuous function
f_{\lambda,n}(t)
such that

f_{\lambda,n}(t):=\begin{cases}1&\text{if \ $t\leq\lambda$},\\
1+n(\lambda-t)&\text{if \ $\lambda\leq t\leq\lambda+n^{-1}$},\\
0&\text{if \ $\lambda+n^{-1}\leq t$}.\\
\end{cases}

Then, there exists a projection
E(\lambda)\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
commuting with
A
such that

E(\lambda)={\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{\lambda,n}(A)

and
\langle h,AE(\lambda)h\rangle\leq\lambda
for all unit vectors
h
.

Proof.

Note that
f_{\lambda,n}(t)\geq f_{\lambda,n+1}(t)\geq 0
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Remark
9.3.3
shows that
\{f_{\lambda,n}(A)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a decreasing sequence of positive operators. Thanks to Lemma
9.3.2
we thus have a positive operator

E(\lambda)
as the strong limit

E(\lambda)={\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{\lambda,n}(A).

Since
f_{\lambda,n}(A)A=Af_{\lambda,n}(A)
, we also have the commutativity of
A
with this strong limit,

E(\lambda)A=AE(\lambda)
.
Further, by Remark
9.3.4
, given a unit vectors
h
,

\langle h,Af_{\lambda,n}(A)h\rangle\leq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}tf_{\lambda,n}(t)

As the supremum tends to
\lambda
as
n\to\infty
, we get

\langle h,AE(\lambda)h\rangle\leq\lambda
for all unit vectors
h
.

Since
1\geq f_{\lambda,n}(t)
, Remark
9.3.3
shows that
I\geq E(\lambda)
and hence
E(\lambda)\geq E^{2}(\lambda)
. We also have

E^{2}(\lambda)\geq E(\lambda)
, indeed,

\displaystyle E^{2}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\left({\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{\lambda,n}(A)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{n\to\infty}f^{2}_{\lambda,n}(A)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{sot}}\lim_{m\to\infty}f_{\lambda,m}(A)=E(\lambda)

as for each integer
n
there exists an integer
m
such that
f^{2}_{\lambda,n}(t)\geq f_{\lambda,m}(t)
.
Thus
E^{2}(\lambda)=E(\lambda)
, i.e.,
E(\lambda)
is a projection.
∎

Remark 9.4.3
.

The projection valued map
\lambda\to E(\lambda)
is increasing and sot right continuous, we call
this map (as well as the family
\{E(\lambda)\}
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R})

the spectral measure
of
A
. The previous lemma shows
that given two self-adjoint operators
A
and
B
with respectives spectral measures
\{E(\lambda)\}
and
\{F(\nu)\}
, then the
commutativity assumption

AB=BA
ensures the commutativity of the spectral measures:
E(\lambda)F(\nu)=F(\nu)E(\lambda)
.

9.5
The spectral theorem

In case of
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
, the matrix decomposition (
9.2.3
) (involving the matrix spectral measure….) remains
valid in a continuous form:

Theorem 9.5.1
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
with its spectral measure
E(\lambda)
. Then

A=\int\lambda\,{\mathrm{d}}E(\lambda)

Now we explain the Stieltjes-integral notation employed, and next give the proof which is a straightforward consequence of the existence
of the spectral measure built up in the previous Lemma.

We may detail a little bit more the theorem, The spectral measure vanishes on an open set
\omega(A)
of
\mathbb{R}
:

x\in\omega(A)\iff\exists\varepsilon>0\ {\mathrm{s.t.}}\ E(x+\varepsilon)-E(x-\varepsilon)=0.

The complementary set of
\omega
is the compact set
\sigma(A)
, the spectrum of
A
. Then we may write the spectral theorem as

Theorem 9.5.2
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
with its spectral measure
E(\lambda)
. Then

A=\int_{\sigma(A)}\lambda\,{\mathrm{d}}E(\lambda).

Denote by
C^{*}(A)
the unital
C^{*}
-algebra spanned by
A
. We have the following concrete version of the Gelfand isomorphism
mentionned in the introduction.

Corollary 9.5.3
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
. Then the functional calculus from
C^{0}(\sigma(A))
to
C^{*}(A)
,

f(t)\mapsto f(A):=\int_{\sigma(A)}f(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}E(\lambda).

is an isometric
*
-isomorphism.

From Theorem
9.5.1
and Remark
9.4.3
we get:

Corollary 9.5.4
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
be commuting. Then
AB\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{H}})
.

Taking advantage that a normal operator
N
has a decomposition
N=A+iB
in which
A
and
B
are two commuting self-adjoint
operators, the above corollary and Remark
9.4.3
show that
A
and
B
have
two commuting respective spectral measures, say
E(x)
and
F(y)
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus we obtain a spectral measure
G(z)
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
, for
N
and get the following spectral theorem for normal operators:

Theorem 9.5.5
.

Let
N
be a normal operator with its spectral measure
G(\lambda)
. Then

N=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\lambda\,{\mathrm{d}}G(\lambda)=\int_{\sigma(N)}\lambda\,{\mathrm{d}}G(\lambda)
